DEFINITIONS 
 
Accessory Dwelling Unit (ADU) - A secondary, self-contained residential unit located on the 
same lot as a primary dwelling, which includes its own kitchen, bathroom and sleeping area and 
is subordinate in size and use to the principal residence. It can be attached as an addition to the 
primary residence or within the basement or detached as a garage apartment or backyard cottage. 
 
Adequate Public Facilities - Adequate Public Facilities means that roads, schools, water, sewer, 
and other public services must be in place and able to support new development before it is built. 
This ensures that infrastructure must be in place and able to support new development before it 
is built. 
 
Affordable Housing - Affordable housing is generally defined as housing on which the occupant 
is paying no more than 30 percent of gross income for housing costs, including utilities. 
 
Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) - A federal law that prohibits discrimination against 
people with physical or mental disabilities in employment, public services, and places of public 
accommodation such as restaurants, hotels, and theaters. 
 
Assisted Living - A supportive housing facility designed for those who need extra help in their 
day-to-day lives, but who do not require the 24-hour skilled nursing care found in traditional 
nursing homes. 
 
Buffer zone - A section of land that reduces the impact between two areas – such as residential, 
commercial, agricultural or industrial uses. The buffer may be required to reduce noise, pollution, 
visual impact, provide privacy or protect natural resources. The buffer can consist of landscape 
plantings or other vegetation, open space, fences, berms, walls or a combination. 
 
Building Restriction Line (BRLs) - Determines the minimum distance a building may be set back 
from the property edge, road, or other feature. 
 
Capital Improvement Program (CIP) - A multi-year plan that identifies large projects, the costs, 
and when they will be built or upgraded. Examples would be building or repairing roads and 
bridges, construction of schools, parks, or other public buildings. 
 
Certificate of Occupancy (CO) - An official document issued by Planning & Codes that certifies 
a building is safe and legally ready to be used or occupied. This is required after new construction, 
major renovations or additions and when there is a change in building use (e.g., from office to 
retail or residential to restaurant). 
 
Detached Dwelling - A free-standing home, typically single-family in nature. 
 
Density - Density is the number of residential dwelling units permitted, usually measured as the

<!-- page 6 -->
v 
 
number of homes/units per acre. Higher density means more homes in a smaller area, while lower 
density means fewer homes spread out over more land. 
 
Historic Preservation - The practice of preserving, protecting, and maintaining buildings, sites, 
and places that have historical, cultural, or architectural importance so that future generations can 
enjoy and learn from them. 
 
Industrial - Industrial areas are places where goods are made, processed, built, or stored such 
as factories, warehouses, or manufacturing plants. 
 
Infill development - A planning approach that creates or expands existing local destinations by 
reactivating underutilized buildings and lots to align with community needs. 
 
Infrastructure - Infrastructure is the underlying framework of facilities and services that support 
everyday life and economic activity. Examples: Physical (hard) infrastructure would be roads, 
power lines, utilities, and bridges with Social (soft) would be schools, healthcare and emergency 
services. 
 
Institutional - Institutional refers to places that provide public or community services such as 
hospitals, schools, post offices, places of worship, police/fire stations and government facilities. 
 
Level of Service (Traffic) - A scale that describes how well a road or intersection is handling 
traffic. It shows whether driving, walking, or biking is easy and free-flowing, or slow and 
congested. It is measured using a combination of data on traffic volume, road capacity, and 
delays then graded based on the Level of Service table or criteria from the Highway Capacity 
Manual (nationally recognized). 
 
Mixed Use - Land use approach that integrates a mix of uses, including residential, commercial, 
pedestrian, and industrial. 
 
Mortgage Burdened - A homeowner who spends 30% or more of their monthly income on their 
mortgage payment. 
 
Multifamily - Multifamily refers to a building or development that contains more than separate 
housing units such as apartments, townhomes, or condominiums. They can be in a single building 
(apartments) or multiple attached buildings (townhomes). 
 
National Register of Historic Districts - The official list, established by the National Historic 
Preservation Act, of site, districts, buildings, structures, and objects significant to the nation’s 
history or whose artistic or architectural value is unique. 
Non-Profit Organization - A non-profit is formed for the purpose of serving a public or mutual 
benefit other than the pursuit or accumulation of profits for owners or investors. 
 
Open Space - Land that is preserved for environmental, recreational, agricultural, or community 
uses. Open space areas are available for active or passive use, including community gardens,

<!-- page 7 -->
vi 
 
parks, recreation areas, forests, wetlands, wildlife habitats, buffers and public gathering spaces. 
 
Renewable Energy - Energy derived from sources that naturally replenish, like sunlight, wind, 
water, and heat from the earth and do not deplete natural resources. Common types include solar, 
wind, hydroelectric, and geothermal. 
 
Rent Burdened - A renter who spends 30% or more of their monthly income on their rental 
payment for housing. 
 
Right-of-Way: 
Public Right-of-Way: Land set aside for roads, sidewalks, or utilities that are owned or managed 
by the government and available for public use. 
Private Right-of-Way: Land that allows access across private property (driveway, easement, or 
private road) but is owned and controlled by a private person or organization. 
 
Setback - Also used like a building restriction line to determine the minimum distance a building 
may be set back from the property edge, road, or other feature. Setbacks prioritize spacing for 
safety and environmental considerations. 
 
Semi-Detached Dwelling - Typically, a residential building containing two dwelling units that 
share one common wall, with each having its own separate entrance and portion of a lot. For 
example, a duplex. 
 
Stormwater (SW) - Stormwater is rain or melted snow that flows over streets, roofs, and other 
surfaces instead of soaking into the ground. It needs to be managed so that it doesn’t cause 
flooding or pollution. 
 
Stormwater Management (SWM) - Stormwater management is the way rainwater or melted 
snow is controlled and directed so it doesn’t cause flooding, erosion, or pollution. Drains, ponds, 
retentions basins, green spaces, and rain gardens are tools used to manage the runoff. Maryland 
has state standards and regulations that must be adopted by counties and municipalities that set 
minimum control requirements. Plans must also be submitted, reviewed and approved as well as 
enforced with maintenance requirements. 
 
Sustainable Development - Planning and building in a way that meets today’s needs without 
comprising the ability of the future generations to meet their own needs. 
 
Transferable Development Rights (TDRs) - This is a program that allows residential 
development potential from land to be moved from one property to another. This helps to protect 
and preserve farmland, open space, or environmentally sensitive areas (sending areas) and direct 
growth to areas better suited for development (receiving areas). Landowners can sell their 
development rights while maintaining ownership of the land. 
 
Zoning - Zoning sets the rules for how land can be divided and to control how it can be used for 
homes, businesses, and industry.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
The Planning and Zoning Enabling Act, previously in Article 66B of the Annotated Code of 
Maryland, was repealed in 2012 and replaced by the Land Use Article of the Maryland Annotated 
Code. It requires that county and municipal plans be implemented through the ordinance, and 
regulations consistent with the Act and its guiding “visions.” 
 
Several changes that impact the comprehensive plan have passed since the 2010 Caroline County 
Comprehensive plan. 
 
In 2012 “The Sustainable Growth and Agricultural Preservation Act of 2012” created statewide 
rules regulating residential development that used individual septic systems. This required 
counties to map land into four “growth tiers” that determined where new housing subdivisions using 
septic systems can be built. Counties and municipalities had to adopt Tier Maps in their 
comprehensive plans and ensure the designations met the criteria set in the law. Caroline County 
amended the Comprehensive Plan and adopted Tier Maps in 2014. 
 
In the 2013 General Assembly session, legislation passed which amended Sections 1-207, 1-416, 
1-509, 3-301 and 3-303 of the Land Use Article, Annotated Code of Maryland. 
 
This legislation changed the review process and cycle of comprehensive plans: 
 
1. Local comprehensive plan and zoning reviews cycles were increased from six years to 10 
years; 
2. All planning commissions and boards are required to file a local annual report with their 
jurisdiction’s legislative body at least once within the five-year period after the adoption or 
review of the local jurisdiction’s comprehensive plan; 
3. Planning in coordination MACO and MML, is directed to create a schedule to transition the 
comprehensive planning cycle of each local jurisdiction from a six-year cycle to a 10-year 
cycle by December 1, 2015, coinciding with the release of census data; and 
4. All jurisdictions that adopted growth tiers, as outlined in 2012 septic law, are required to 
incorporate their growth tier map into its comprehensive plan at the time the jurisdiction 
was scheduled to conduct its six-year comprehensive plan review prior to the effective 
date of this legislation. Growth tiers that are not incorporated into the comprehensive plan 
will no longer be considered adopted. 
 
In the 2015 session, legislation passed that amended Sections 3-204 and 3-205 of the Land Use 
Article, Annotated Code of Maryland. 
 
This legislation changed the authority of a local legislative body for a non-charter county or 
municipality to adopt, amend or reject a comprehensive plan submitted by its planning 
commission:

<!-- page 11 -->
x 
 
1. The legislative body of a non-charter county or municipality is authorized to adopt, modify, 
remand or disapprove all or part of a comprehensive plan submitted by its planning 
commission; 
2. The legislative body is required to hold a public hearing before adopting or modifying the plan 
and authorizes the body to hold a public hearing before remanding or disapproving the plan; 
3. The planning commission is required to hold a public hearing before submitting a new 
recommended plan to the legislative body, if the body remanded or disapproved the prior 
submitted plan; 
4. The local legislative body is required to approve, modify, remand or disapprove a submitted 
plan within 90 days after receipt, otherwise the recommendation of the planning commission 
shall be considered approved; and 
5. The legislative body may extend the 90-day public hearing deadline up to an additional 60 days 
if there are pressing circumstances preventing the body from acting on the plan within 90 days. 
 
In 2020, the Covid-19 pandemic impacted everyone and had a profound effect on the County 
during the development of this plan, resulting in many changes – some positive and others more 
challenging. In addition to the standard review process and requirements for a Comprehensive 
Plan, additional considerations were incorporated to build upon the positive outcome and mitigate 
adverse impacts. This plan, along with subsequent regulations and policies, is intended to 
promote recovery and strengthen community resilience. 
 
The purpose of the plan is to guide growth toward existing population centers; protect and ensure 
adequate water supply; preserve agricultural, heritage and natural resources; maintain 
appropriate community facilities that meet the needs of a changing population; improve 
transportation safety and accessibility through coordinated planning and expanded public and 
alternative travel options; promote economic development and employment opportunities; and 
encourage the redevelopment of existing housing while supporting affordable housing initiatives. 
 
This comprehensive plan is essential to ensure that Caroline County is well-prepared for future 
growth within both the County and its municipalities. With a projected population of 35,240 County 
residents by 2035, a significant shift in the average age of the population is expected. The largest 
age groups in the County based on the 2020 U.S. Census, are 60-64 (older working and early 
senior), 5-9 (younger school age) and 35-39 (working). By 2035, the largest age group will be 
seniors and the second largest will be young adults just out of high school or college. These age 
groups will shape the types of services and amenities the County provides over the next 10 years 
related to an aging population’s needs and a significant impact on the schools and workforce 
demands. A broad and evolving range of demographic and workforce challenges must be 
addressed over the next ten years. 
 
Additionally, the County has experienced notable growth in its Hispanic/Latino population over 
the past decade, underscoring the importance of developing public services and community 
programs that promote inclusion, accessibility, and meaningful engagement.

<!-- page 12 -->
xi 
 
The adoption of this Comprehensive Plan will influence future development and growth in the 
County; however, it is important to understand the limitations of this document. While subsequent 
regulations, plans, and policies must be consisted with the adopted plan, the Comprehensive Plan 
itself is not a regulatory tool. It is a policy setting document used to update the Zoning Code and 
Subdivision Regulations, and to guide County programs and budgets. This is a long-range plan 
that will be updated again in 2036. 
 
Implementation of strategies and performance measures are included in the Comprehensive Plan 
to identify responsible departments and to track progress over time. 
 
The Comprehensive Plan is not a stand-alone document, but is supported (and, in turn supports) 
related planning program documents including, but not limited to: 
 
• 
Chesapeake Bay Critical Area Plan 
• 
Comprehensive Water & Sewer Plan 
• 
LLPPR 
• 
Floodplain Ordinance 
• 
Zoning Code 
• 
Subdivision Regulations 
• 
Septic Tier Maps (SB 236) 
• 
Road Ordinance 
• 
Forest Conservation Ordinance 
• 
Building Code 
• 
Livability Code 
• 
Stormwater Management Ordinance 
 
This plan meets the 12 Visions required for Maryland comprehensive plans (prior to 2025) which 
are the statewide goals that local plans must address and implement. 
 
1. Quality of Life and Sustainability – Achieve a high quality of life through stewardship of 
land, water, and air, resulting in sustainable communities and environmental protection. 
2. Public Participation – Ensure citizen are active partners in planning and implementation. 
3. Growth Areas – Concentrate growth in existing population and business center or 
strategically selected new center. 
4. Community Design – Encourage compact, mixed-use, walkable design consistent with 
community character and near transit options. 
5. Infrastructure – Provide appropriate water resources and infrastructure to support orderly, 
efficient, and environmentally sound growth. 
6. Transportation – Support a safe, convenient, affordable, and efficient multimodal 
transportation system. 
7. Housing - Provide a range of housing densities, types, and sizes for all ages and incomes. 
8. Economic Development – Promote economic development with employment opportunities 
for all income levels within the community’s resource and service capacity.

<!-- page 13 -->
xii 
 
9. Environmental Protection - Manage land and water resources to restore and maintain 
healthy air, water, natural systems, and living resources. 
10. Resource Conservation – Conserve waterways, open space, natural systems, scenic 
areas, forests, and agricultural lands. 
11. Stewardship – Encourage collaboration among government, business, and residents to 
balance growth with resource protection. 
12. Implementation – Integrate strategies, policies, programs, and funding across all levels to 
achieve these visions. 
 
On April 8, 2025, through House Bill 286, the 12 Planning Visions were replaced with eight 
principles to guide the state’s shift from Smart Growth to Sustainable Growth. All new 
Comprehensive Plans must meet these eight principles. As Caroline County has been working 
on the Comprehensive Plan for the last several years this plan is focused on the 12 Planning 
Visions and the next comprehensive plan in 2036 must adhere to the eight new principles which 
are: 
 
1. Land – Optimize productivity of working landscapes including farms and forests and 
fisheries and prioritize development within population centers that are in proximity to 
existing infrastructure. 
2. Transportation – Prioritize transportation networks that create energy efficient, affordable, 
and reliable access to jobs, housing and services. 
3. Housing – Enable a mix of quality housing types and affordability options to accommodate 
all who want to live in the state. 
4. Economy – Allow for adaptive reuse, mixed-use and context appropriate new development 
that responds to changing markets and innovations. 
5. Equity – Engage all sectors of the community in plan development to ensure diverse 
voices are heard and the needs of underserved populations are prioritized. 
6. Resilience - Integrate resiliency measures that will minimize the impacts of rapid and 
unexpected natural – and human -caused threats on communities. 
7. Place - Provide public spaces that encourage social interaction and value cultural, 
historical and natural resources. 
8. Ecology – Protect and restore sensitive ecological systems and conserve natural 
resources, including forests, agricultural areas, and waterways. 
 
Although not required, this plan does meet the Sustainable Growth Planning Principles that were 
enacted for Comprehensive Plans in 2025 by promoting responsible growth, protecting natural 
and mineral resources, and guiding development into compact, walkable communities. It identifies 
the limited housing options and outlines strategies to expand opportunities, coordinated 
transportation and infrastructure systems, and economic options that align with the community’s 
capacity. Through extensive public engagement, regional coordination, and clear implementation 
strategies, the Plan balances environmental stewardship, resilience, and quality of life, fully 
reflecting the intent of the 2025 Sustainable Growth framework.

<!-- page 14 -->
1 
 
 
Why Plan: 
 
Planning enables the County to protect public health, safety and welfare by anticipating future 
population needs and recommending strategies to meet them. The Caroline County 
Commissioners have long prioritized preserving the County’s agricultural and rural character, 
along with protecting water resources and other natural features, and addressing community 
needs related to housing, transportation, and economic development. 
 
The 2026 Caroline County Comprehensive Plan replaces the 2010 Plan but builds on this 
longstanding philosophy, introducing more proactive measures to further protect prime 
agricultural lands and maintain the rural landscape. Growth and development should generally 
be directed to incorporated municipalities where public services and infrastructure are already in 
place. This plan provides a strong framework for guiding targeted development, safeguarding 
water and natural resources, and addressing future challenges through thoughtful strategic 
planning. 
 
In addition, the Comprehensive Plan serves as an important tool for coordination and decision-
making across all levels of the County. It establishes a consistent reference for zoning, land use 
regulations, capital improvements, and public investments, ensuring that individual actions 
contribute to broader community goals. By engaging residents, stakeholders, and local officials 
in the planning process, the plan reflects shared priorities and fosters transparency and 
accountability. It also positions the County to respond effectively to changing conditions, such as 
population shifts, economic trends, and environmental challenges, while maintaining its 
commitment to preserving the rural character and enhancing the overall quality of life. 
 
Plan Elements: 
 
Land Use 
 
The Land Use Element establishes a strategic 
direction for growth. It calls for updating zoning 
districts to align with the Plan’s visions and 
development patterns. Growth will be directed to 
incorporated municipalities where infrastructure and 
public services exist, while preserving the rural 
character of unincorporated areas. To discourage 
sprawl, the County will work with the municipalities to 
expand public sewer where feasible. 
 
The County recognizes that certain land uses, including large-scale solar facilities, have State 
INTRODUCTION

<!-- page 15 -->
2 
 
regulations that may supersede local goals and regulations. While supporting renewable energy, 
the County remains committed to protecting prime farmland and high-value agricultural lands to 
avoid long-term impacts on the County’s agricultural economy, agritourism opportunities, and 
the local and regional food supply. 
 
Resource Conservation 
 
This element aims to protect the County’s natural and 
heritage resources while maintaining an agricultural 
land base that supports our farming community and 
local agricultural economy. 
It promotes best-
management 
practices 
to 
safeguard 
natural 
resources, strengthens agricultural land preservation 
efforts, and protects historic sites and structures to 
encourage heritage tourism. 
 
Since 2010, State legislation (SB931/HB 1036 effective July 1, 2025) has expanded approvals 
for large-scale solar facilities on farmland and Priority Preservation Areas (PPAs), limiting local 
zoning authority. The law caps solar development at 5% of a county’s PPA – approximately 
6,350 of 127,000 PPA acres within Caroline County – after which additional local regulatory 
control may apply. The Maryland Agricultural Land Preservation Foundation (MALPF) continues 
to acquire easements, preserving thousands of acres. The County will proactively establish 
policies to ensure solar development on agricultural/heritage lands to protect farming, heritage 
tourism, natural-resource stewardship, and long-term land preservation. 
 
Water Resources 
 
The Water Resources element identifies drinking 
water and other resources needed to support 
current and future development and outlines 
strategies to reduce nutrient pollution. While water 
quantity 
is 
generally 
sufficient, 
long-term 
sustainability remains a concern given the County’s 
agricultural character and potential future demand 
from data centers. Ongoing state and private 
studies continue to assess water availability and 
potential stressors. 
 
To promote sustainability, the County works with stakeholder agencies to obtain current data on 
water availability, projected needs, and guide groundwater management. Water quality initiatives 
include wastewater treatment upgrades, agricultural best management practices (BMPs) such as

<!-- page 16 -->
3 
 
nutrient management plans and cover crops, and enforcement of stormwater and erosion controls 
for new development. The County also participates in state-led watershed initiatives, including 
the Phase II and Phase III Watershed Implementation Plans (WIPs) to meet Total Maximum Daily 
Load (TMDL) requirements for nutrient and sediment reduction targets, with preliminary results 
showing measurable progress. 
 
Moving forward, the County will continue monitoring nutrient loads, groundwater levels, saltwater 
intrusion and overall water resource sustainability. Future planning will rely on updated data from 
state agencies, ongoing monitoring programs, and continued collaboration with agricultural 
stakeholders to balance growth, agricultural, productivity, and environmental protection. These 
efforts will ensure a safe, reliable, and sustainable water supply while protecting the County’s 
agricultural economy, natural resources and long-term resilience. 
 
Mineral Resources 
 
The Mineral Resource Element identifies and 
addresses Caroline County’s assets, including 
sand, gravel, and other materials that support 
local construction, infrastructure, and economic 
activity. The County balances the extraction of 
these resources with environmental protection, 
community health, and the preservation of 
agricultural and natural lands. 
 
Mining operations are regulated under state and local laws to ensure responsible practices, 
including site planning, traffic management, water quality protection, dust and noise control, and 
land reclamation. Permits and ongoing compliance monitoring help protect surrounding 
communities, roadways, and natural resources. 
 
The County promotes best management practices and reclamation of mined lands for future 
agricultural, recreational, or conservation use. Moving forward, mineral resource development 
will continue to support economic growth, but with regulations in place to protect the County’s 
rural character, natural resources, and overall environmental quality. 
 
Priority Preservation Area 
The Priority Preservation Area 
(PPA) element is required under 
the Agricultural Stewardship Act 
of 2006 (HB2), for counties 
seeing certification under the 
Maryland 
Agricultural 
Land 
Preservation 
Foundation 
(MALPF) program.

<!-- page 17 -->
4 
 
Certification allows the County to retain 75% of the locally generated agricultural land transfer tax 
(rather than the standard 33 1/3%) which provides critical local matching funds for easement 
acquisition and other preservation tools. 
 
The County’s designated PPA includes approximately 127,000 acres, including all unincorporated 
land not designated as a TDR receiving areas, municipal growth area, or State Priority Funday 
Areas. As of February 2026, 37,948 acres have been preserved with permanent easements and 
1048.86 acres via the County’s TDR program. 
 
As interest in solar energy development increases, careful planning within the PPA is necessary 
to protect long-term agricultural viability. County policymakers will review all options to ensure 
farmland is being preserved while supporting renewable energy goals. 
 
Community Facilities 
 
For the purpose of this plan, community facilities in 
the County include the following: recreation and 
parks facilities, educational institutions, libraries, 
emergency and public health services facilities, 
courthouses and legal services centers, correctional 
facilities, airports, and public works facilities. The 
goals of this chapter are to ensure that these 
facilities continue to meet the evolving needs of 
County residents while supporting the County’s 
broader land use and growth management goals. 
The key proposals focus on directing growth toward 
existing population centers to improve public access 
to facilities and services and to strengthen 
coordination among the Board of Education, 
Recreation and Parks, Emergency Services and 
other service providers. 
 
Transportation 
The transportation element outlines the County’s highway 
needs, 
planned 
improvements 
and 
planned 
improvements, including upcoming bridge construction 
and repair projects. It also addresses public transit and 
alternative transportation options. As the County’s elderly 
population continues to grow, public transportation will 
play an increasingly vital role. Expanding bicycle routes 
and incorporating alternative transportation plans within 
designated growth areas will support heritage tourism and 
guide new development toward these targeted areas.

<!-- page 18 -->
5 
 
Economic Development 
 
Major economic development priorities in Caroline 
County include strengthening regional infrastructure 
and services, achieving economies of scale, 
expanding 
tourism 
opportunities and creating 
opportunities for new industry. Key implementation 
goals will be evaluation of zoned land in appropriate 
locations for new commercial, industrial and 
institutional uses during the comprehensive 
rezoning process; updating home-based business 
regulations to promote economic development while minimizing the impacts on neighboring 
properties; supporting heritage tourism and expanding tourism opportunities throughout the 
County. Evaluation of surface mining performance and site mitigation standards will also be 
evaluated. 
 
The element also recommends guiding unincorporated growth to areas near municipalities and 
improving infrastructure and public services to support that growth. Municipal expansion can 
attract new employers, stimulate local downtown businesses, and increase demand for service 
industries. It may also strengthen the agricultural economy by boosting the market for locally 
grown produce and locally crafted goods. 
 
Housing 
 
The goal of the housing chapter is to ensure that 
all residents of Caroline County have access to 
affordable, safe, and sanitary housing. Based on 
2016-2020 5-year estimates from the U.S. 
Census 
Bureau, 
approximately 
34.9% 
of 
households in the County spent 30% or more of 
their income on housing costs. 
Affordable 
housing is defined under Maryland law as housing 
costing no more than 30% of a household’s 
monthly income. 
 
Key housing implementation goals include increasing participation by both County and municipal 
representatives on the County Housing Advisory Board, reviewing and updating livability codes 
to ensure safe and sound housing conditions, encouraging the renovation and adaptive reuse of 
older housing stock, and expanding support –through programs such as the County Housing 
Rehabilitation Program – to maintain and repair aging structures. 
 
Recent statewide legislation advances these objectives. The landmark law SB 891/HB1466 
(effective October 1, 2025) establishes a state policy to promote the development of accessory 
dwelling units (ADUs) on lots with single -family detached homes and requires all Maryland

<!-- page 19 -->
6 
 
jurisdictions to adopt local laws authorizing ADUs by October 1, 2026. Caroline County currently 
allows ADUs and will provide updated requirements with the update of the Zoning Code. The 
Housing Expansion and Affordability Act (SB 484/HB 538) removes barriers to housing 
construction, modernizes land-use regulations, and promotes development in transit-oriented and 
other strategic locations. Complementing this, the Housing and Community Development 
Financing Act (SB483/HB 599) expands state housing finance tools, including the creation of the 
Maryland Community Investment Corporation to channel investment into housing and community 
development projects. These laws support a broader range of housing types - including ADUs 
and factory-built/modular homes - and align with the County’s goal to diversify housing options 
and reduce cost burdens. 
 
Maryland Smart Growth Act (1997) 
 
The Neighborhood Conservation and Smart Growth Areas Act (Smart Growth Act) directs 
Maryland to focus funding for growth-related projects within designated Priority Funding Areas 
(PFAs) while protecting agricultural and natural resource lands. 
 
Key provisions still in effect: 
 
• 
Priority Funding Areas (PFAs): The State may fund major growth-related projects – such as 
transportation, water and sewer infrastructure, and economic development – primarily within 
PFAs. 
• 
Automatic Municipal Designation: All municipalities existing as of January 1, 1997, are 
automatically designated as PFAs. 
• 
Annexation Requirements: Areas annexed after January 1, 1997, must meet additional 
density, planning, and infrastructure criteria to qualify as PFAs. 
• 
Local Planning Requirements: Counties and municipalities must adopt comprehensive plans 
and zoning ordinances that: 
o Direct growth to designated areas 
o Meet minimum density standards 
o Ensure adequate public facilities 
o Align with the County’s 10-year Water and Sewerage Plan 
• 
State Certification Process: Municipal annexations must be reviewed and certified through 
coordination with county and state agencies, including the Maryland Department of Planning, 
to receive PFA status and related funding. 
 
Adequate Public Facilities (APF) Laws 
 
Adequate public facilities (APF) laws allow Maryland counties and municipalities to manage the pace 
and location of growth by requiring that essential infrastructure – such as schools, roads, 
water/sewer systems, parks, and emergency services – be available and adequate before new 
development is approved.

<!-- page 20 -->
7 
 
Originally authorized in 1978 under Article 66B Section 10.01, this authority was recodified in 2012 
into the Land Use Article §4-301 of the Maryland Code as part of a statewide reorganization of 
planning and zoning laws. The statute applies to non-charter counties and municipalities. 
 
APF laws are intended to prevent development where public facilities are inadequate and phase or 
delay development in planned areas until adequate public services are available. These laws support 
local comprehensive plans and must include clear, measurable standards, aligning with Maryland’s 
Planning & Zoning Enabling Act. 
 
Maryland has strengthened the broader planning framework within which APF operates. Key 
updates include: 
 
• 
2011 Plan Maryland and Sustainable Growth policies: Reinforced coordination between local 
land use decisions and state investment priorities. 
• 
2013 Sustainable Growth and Agricultural Preservation Act (SB236): Added limits on major 
residential subdivisions in certain rural areas without public sewer, complementing APF by 
further managing growth location. 
 
There is continued emphasis that APF ordinances must contain clear, objective, and measurable 
standards to comply with Maryland’s Planning & Zoning Enabling Act and avoid arbitrary application. 
 
Comprehensive Plan Public Participation 
 
In accordance with the Maryland Land Use Article (§1-416 and §3-303), a public hearing must be held 
by the Planning Commission before a comprehensive plan is adopted or amended. Public notice of 
the hearing must be published in a newspaper of general circulation once a week for two consecutive 
weeks, with the first notice appearing at least 14 days prior to the hearing. The notice must include 
the date, time and location of the hearing, a brief summary of the proposed plan, and information on 
where the draft plan can be reviewed by the public. 
 
Additionally, a copy of the draft comprehensive plan must be submitted to the Maryland Department 
of Planning at least 60 days prior to the public hearing. It is strongly encouraged that jurisdictions also 
post notice on their official website, notify adjacent jurisdictions and key stakeholders, and make the 
plan available through libraries and community centers to encourage public engagement and 
participation.

Background and History 
 
Caroline County is located on the Delmarva Peninsula in the State of Maryland and is part of the 
Upper Eastern Shore Region. This region comprises five counties; Caroline, Cecil, Kent, Queen 
Anne’s, and Talbot. The County is bordered by Queen Anne’s, Talbot, and Dorchester Counties in 
Maryland and by Kent and Sussex Counties in the State of Delaware. There are ten incorporated 
municipalities in Caroline County: Denton (the County Seat), Federalsburg, Goldsboro, Greensboro, 
Henderson, Hillsboro, Marydel, Preston, Ridgely, and Templeville. 
 
Caroline is a “Code Home Rule” county under Maryland law, granting it broad local legislative 
authority. The County is governed by three elected County Commissioners, who serve four-year 
terms. 
 
Caroline County was established in 1773 from parts of Dorchester and Queen Anne’s Counties by 
Maryland’s last colonial governor, Robert Eden. The County was named after Caroline Calvert, wife to 
Robert Eden and the sister of Frederick Calvert, the last Lord Baltimore. At the time of its creation, 
seven commissioners were appointed: Charles Dickinson, Benson Stainton, Thomas White, William 
Haskins, Richard Mason, Joshua Clark, and Nathaniel Potter. 
 
• 
T. Alan Goldsborough (Denton): Served as Caroline County State’s Attorney (1904-1908), 
U.S. Representative (1921-1939), and was appointed by Franklin D. Roosevelt as a judge on 
the U.S. District Court in Washington, DC, where he presided over high-profile labor cases 
involving John L. Lewis. 
• 
Sherman W. Tribbitt (Denton): Served as Governor of Delaware from 1973-1977. 
• 
Harry R. Hughes (Denton): Served as Governor of Maryland from 1979-1987. His 
administration was known for pioneering environmental protections, most notably the Critical 
Area Act of 1984, which addressed land use impacts on the Chesapeake Bay and became a 
cornerstone of Maryland’s environmental policy. 
• 
Wayne Calwey, Jr (Denton): Longest serving Secretary of Agriculture who served from 1979-
1992 and later lent his name to the department’s headquarters. 
 
Agriculture has long been the driving economic, social and political force in Caroline County. During 
the agrarian movement of the late 1800s and early 1900s, the Democratic Party relied on local farmers 
for crucial support, and Caroline has since continued to back leaders who advocate for agricultural 
interest. From 1920 to 1990, the County thrived with canneries, grain brokerages, and trucking firms 
fueled by large-scale farming. In 2015, Caroline County ranked as a major agricultural county in 
Maryland, leading the state in barley, wheat and vegetable production. Caroline County is among the 
top counties in Maryland for poultry production and poultry farms. At the state level for 2017, in the 
category “Poultry & Eggs”, Caroline County was ranked number one in Maryland for market value of 
agricultural products sold.

<!-- page 23 -->
10 
 
 
Caroline County agricultural sales continue to rank high in the State and nation, including in the 
following categories (from the 2022 Maryland Census of Agriculture): 
 
• 
Poultry and eggs:  
o #2 in Maryland; #67 in U.S. (top 2%) – 2022 sales $263M 
• 
Grains, oilseeds, dry beans, dry peas:  
o #3 in Maryland; #746 in U.S. (top 25%) – 2022 sales $77M  
• 
Vegetables, melons, potatoes, sweet potatoes:  
o #2 in Maryland; #184 in U.S. (top 6%)– 2022 $17M 
 
Caroline County remains strongly committed to agriculture as its primary industry, even as suburban 
development pressures increase along with the mandates for large solar installations. Agricultural 
preservation continues to be one of the County’s highest priorities, supported by multiple tools 
including a Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) program that directs growth to designated 
receiving areas. The County is working to strengthen this program by coordinating sending and 
receiving areas with its municipalities. The Growth Tier Maps, adopted in 2012, also limit and define 
areas for development and for preservation: 
 
Tier I – Areas within existing public sewer service and all development will be served by public sewer. 
Tier II – Areas that are planned to be served by public sewer service. Only minor residential 
subdivisions are permitted if the development is not connected to public sewer. 
Tier III – Areas that are not planned for public sewer service. The Planning Commission must hold 
at least one public hearing prior to approval for any major residential subdivisions within the Tier III 
area. 
Tier IV – Preservation and Conservation areas. No major residential subdivisions are permitted, only 
minor residential subdivisions on septic systems. 
 
The recent policy updates and growing interest in solar energy have resulted in the County reviewing 
current regulations and proposed revisions. Solar siting is also tied to the Growth Tier Map, which 
restricts utility-scale solar in Tier 1 and Tier II area and limits the extent of solar facilities with the 
County’s Preservation Area to 5% of the area with the PFAs. Caroline County is reviewing additional 
options and regulations for solar installations to ensure that. Although development and cultural 
changes are now encroaching on Caroline County, agriculture remains its central industry and 
preserving farmland continues to be one of its highest priorities. 
 
The County has adopted several strategies to safeguard its rural character, including limiting new 
major subdivisions in rural zones and directing growth toward designated areas better suited for 
development. Looking ahead, Caroline County intends to strengthen these efforts by working closely 
with municipalities to coordinate land use planning, adequate public facilities, and ensure that growth 
is balanced with long-term agricultural preservation. The Transfer of Development Rights program is 
one of many tools established to preserve agricultural land and direct growth to designated receiving 
areas.

<!-- page 24 -->
11 
 
Caroline County also has abundant natural resources related to its waterways and forested lands. 
The major watersheds in Caroline County are Lower Choptank, Marshyhope Creek, Nanticoke 
River, Tuckahoe Creek and Upper Choptank. A plan for the Upper Choptank River and Tuckahoe 
Creek Watershed Characterizations was completed in November 2007. This plan is used to 
implement protection plans for the Upper Choptank watershed. Protecting and preserving 
agriculture, natural resources and the rural and scenic countryside of the County are among the 
County’s highest priorities. 
 
Population and Growth 
 
Between 1990 and 2020, the County’s population grew from 27,125 to 33,293 – an increase of 
6,168 residents, or 22.75% over those three decades. This trend underscores the County’s 
growing appeal, driven by suburban expansion and migration into the County. From 1990 to 2000 
the population grew from 27,125 to 29,773 – a 9.76% increase. Growth continued from 2000-
2010 with an increase of 3,293 residents or 11.06% in that timeframe. 2010-2020 had a small 
increase in population to 33,293 or .90%.  
 
From 2010-2020, the housing market was still recovering from the 2008 financial crisis, home 
values were depressed in many areas, construction of new homes slowed significantly after 2008, 
and inventory remained low for much of that decade. When housing supply is limited, population 
growth slows.  
 
Based on the 2010 to 2020 Census data, the annual growth rate for the entire Eastern Shore was 
0.17% per year while the annual growth rate for Caroline County was 0.07%. Maryland’s statewide 
growth of 0.7% during that timeframe shows that the state continued to grow but at a slower rate 
compared to previous years and decades. These numbers reflect Caroline County’s stable rural 
character and strong emphasis on agricultural preservation while limiting large-scale suburban 
expansion.

One key factor is the lack of water and/or wastewater treatment facilities in certain towns. This 
infrastructure deficit can deter residential and commercial development, pushing developers 
toward municipalities that already have modern, well-maintained utility systems. As a result, 
towns with limited infrastructure struggle to attract new commercial and residential projects, 
accelerating population loss. 
 
Other contributing factors may include: 
 
• 
Limited economic opportunities: Smaller or declining towns may lack jobs, especially in 
trades that attract younger workers or families. 
• 
Aging populations: Many Eastern Shore towns have disproportionate demographics, with 
the majority being an older and aging population. This leads to natural population decline 
through aging and lower birth rates. 
• 
Outmigration of youth: Younger residents often leave for education or better employment

<!-- page 26 -->
13 
 
opportunities and may not return. 
• 
Housing constraints: The existing housing stock may be outdated or insufficient for modern 
demands. Combined with limited public infrastructure, new residential growth is limited. 
• 
Transportation: Towns with limited access to major highways or transit systems may 
struggle to remain connected to regional economic centers. Rural areas face the challenge of 
limited public transportation options which can contribute to residents relocating for better 
opportunities for employment, education and healthcare. 
• 
COVID-19 pandemic: This caused higher death rates among aging populations, reduced 
birth rates due to economic and social uncertainty, and greater mobility as remote work 
enabled people to relocate to urban or more desirable areas. Additionally, residential 
development slowed or stalled in many small towns due to labor shortages, supply chain 
disruptions, and reduced demand, further limiting population growth and recovery. 
 
The region’s growth patterns indicate that infrastructure, economic opportunity, and demographic 
shifts play critical roles in shaping municipal trajectories. While some towns leverage these factors 
to grow, others – especially smaller or rural municipalities without essential services – face 
ongoing challenges in maintaining or increasing population levels. Understanding these dynamics 
is imperative moving forward for policymakers and local leaders to proactively address needs, 
allocate resources effectively, and design resilient, inclusive strategies that support sustainable 
growth and community well-being. As the County formulates plans and regulations for growth, 
working with the towns will also be essential for the overall community throughout the entire 
County. 
 
According to the 2020 Census, the largest age group in Caroline County was the 55-64 age group 
followed closely by children aged 5-9. The shift in the largest age group seems to be a result of 
the overall population growth in Caroline County. This shift in future years will put additional stress 
on medical facilities, long-term care facilities, and accessible public transportation as the 
population ages, underscoring the importance of forward-looking planning and investment in 
healthcare and mobility resources. 
 
The younger age group will put additional stress on the school capacity, need for additional 
teachers, school programs, recreation spaces and youth services. As the older population’s 
housing needs change, communities for aging residents and housing that is manageable while 
maintaining independence will be desired. As the next generation enters adulthood, the 
availability of starter homes, affordable housing, and local job opportunities will be essential. 
There will be an increased need for more healthcare providers, expanded medical facilities and 
services to serve residents of all ages.

is 73% of the County’s total population, compared to 82% of the total County’s population in 2000.

15% of the population. The population of Caucasians and African Americans in the County has 
declined since 2010, while the categories of “Some other Race” and “Two or more Races” have 
increased dramatically. Additionally, the number of inhabitants claiming Hispanic and Latino 
origins increased by 55% between 2010 and 2020.

Population Projections 
 
The average annual growth rate for Caroline County declined from 1.4% during 1988-2008 to 
.57% between 2000-2020. Several factors contributed to this slowdown. Economic and 
employment shifts in the early 2000s played a major role, including a decline in manufacturing 
jobs and limited growth in employment opportunities. Demographic changes have also influenced 
growth, as the County has experienced an aging population and lower birth rates. While there 
has been some in-migration, out-migration increased with younger residents pursuing education 
and employ opportunities in metropolitan areas. 
 
A key factor has been the housing market. The 2008 financial crisis led to a slowdown in 
development and economic activity with the subsequent recession dampening growth prospects. 
The housing crisis persists as state regulations and insufficient infrastructure limit new residential 
development even with the passing of recent housing legislation. 
 
These combined factors resulted in a noticeable decline in Caroline County’s average annual 
population growth from 2000-2020.

The projected County growth affects water consumption, availability of private or public 
septic/sewer, schools, recreation land for parks and recreational facilities, public safety facilities, 
as well as other public services and amenities such as libraries and transportation. The growing 
population and the aging population will increase the need for diverse housing options and 
additional healthcare facilities. 
 
Based on industry standards for forecasting demand, the estimates for school enrollments will be 
5,598 in 2025, 5,472 in 2030 with estimated enrollment by 2035 to be 5,600-5,800 students. The 
projected enrollment does dip driven by lower birth rates. Based on the projected population by 
2040, it is estimated that the County will need a total of 60 police personnel (1.5 officers per 1,000 
residents for lower crime and rural areas) and 48 (1.2 per 1,000 residents) fire and rescue 
personnel with up to 30,000 square feet in fire and rescue facilities dependent on the number of 
facilities, locations, volunteer and career members, sleeping facilities, apparatus bays, storage 
and training space. A total of 825 acres of recreational land will be required and the recommended 
square footage of library space for the projected 2040 population of 40,000 at 1 square foot per 
capita would be 40,000 square feet. 
 
New Home Construction 
 
Between 2010 and 2025, approximately 711 new housing units were constructed in Caroline 
County, not including replacement homes. Of those new homes, almost 30% were located in 
municipalities and 70% were located in the County. This averages to about 50 homes per year. 
 
Between 2010-2025, new residential development in Caroline County occurred predominately in 
the unincorporated area, reflecting development patterns similar to those seen prior to 2000. This 
trend is largely the result of limited access and availability of public infrastructure and services 
necessary to support development within municipal boundaries. This shift and concentration of 
development in rural areas are inconsistent with the County’s long-term objective of preserving 
farmland and maintaining its rural character. To achieve a more sustainable growth pattern, the 
County will need to prioritize strategic infrastructure investments and coordinated planning efforts 
that direct future residential development toward designated growth areas within municipalities. 
The ongoing housing shortage underscores the need for more diverse, affordable, and accessible 
housing options. There is a critical need to expand the supply of affordable and workforce housing 
to meet current and future demand.

Transportation 
 
Major highway access routes near or within Caroline County include US Route 301, US Route 
50, US Route 13, and MD Route 404. MD Routes serving the County include 16, 404, 480, 311, 
312, 317, and 328. Every major city within the Mid-Atlantic region is located less than 300 miles 
from the County. The closest regional cities include Dover and Wilmington, Delaware; 
Philadelphia, PA; Annapolis, Salisbury and Baltimore in Maryland; and Washington D.C., all 
located within 2 hours driving time of the County. 
 
Geography, Resources, and Industry 
 
Caroline County is approximately 321 square miles or 206,719 acres. According to the Maryland 
Geological Survey, the County elevation ranges from 0 to 79 feet above sea level and is located 
entirely with the Atlantic Coastal Plain, a geographical area extending along the East Coast 
seaboard below New York and Pennsylvania. The topography of the region is relatively flat, which 
has created an environment suitable for crop farming. Large mineral deposits of sand and gravel 
exist in the middle and southern portions of the County.

<!-- page 31 -->
18 
 
Caroline County contains numerous natural resource areas, including large, forested areas, large 
areas of wetlands, a number of rivers and streams, with the major water resources being the 
Choptank and Tuckahoe Rivers and Marshyhope Creek. The County is served by several large 
freshwater aquifers, mainly the Columbia Aquifer widely used for agriculture, domestic wells, and 
small-scale water supply and the Piney Point Aquifer which is a deeper, confined aquifer and one 
of the major water sources for the County.

<!-- page 32 -->
19 
 
Caroline County, the Green Garden County, is defined by its rural landscape of farms and forests. 
The County’s primary land use goal is to preserve its rural and agricultural land by continuing to 
direct  high-density development to incorporated municipalities and areas in the County that are 
zoned and planned for development. These include the R-1 Residential R-2 Residential, VN 
Village Neighborhood and VC Village Center, zoning districts, Priority Funding Areas, municipal 
growth areas and the County’s Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) receiving area.  
The Caroline County Zoning Ordinance is the County’s most effective tool for implementing its 
long-range land use goals and objectives. The County will undertake a comprehensive update of 
the Zoning Ordinance following the adoption of this Comprehensive Plan to implement the goals 
and objectives outlined throughout the Plan.     
The County’s land use objectives to achieve its primary land use goal include the following: 
• 
Protect land uses and zoning districts from State mandates that pre-empt local regulations 
and do not support County land use and development goals. 
• 
Provide comprehensive planning and regulatory mechanisms that protect agricultural land use 
while ensuring orderly and sustainable future growth.  
• 
Protect the R-Rural zoning district from encroachment by non-agricultural land uses that 
threaten the County’s growth and development goals and agriculture-based economy. 
• 
Preserve Residential and Village zoning districts for existing and future residential and mixed-
use development.    
• 
Conserve valuable natural, historical, cultural, archeological and scenic resources and ensure 
they are protected from solar development and surface mining impacts. 
• 
Strengthen County and Municipal coordination through the development of “Inter- 
Governmental Agreements” that coordinate County and Municipal land use, land 
preservation, growth management, and infrastructure goals. 
Existing Land Use and Zoning 
The County’s unincorporated area totals 199,854 acres, or 97% of the County’s total land area. 
Incorporated municipalities total 3% of the County’s total land area. As shown on Map 2-1 Existing 
Land Use, the two dominant land uses in the unincorporated area of the County are agriculture 
and forest, which together comprise 93% of the total land acreage of the County.

<!-- page 34 -->
21 
 
The majority of the unincorporated area of Caroline County, approximately 60% (117,524.41 
acres), is used primarily for agricultural purposes. Agricultural land uses are permitted in the R-
Rural zoning district along with low and very low-density residential use. The R-Rural district is 
the County’s largest zoning district (see Map 2-* Zoning). Agricultural land use includes cropland, 
pastureland and timber woodland. Most of the agricultural land in Caroline County is cropland 
used for soybean, corn and wheat production.  Poultry growing operations in Caroline County and 
the Delmarva Peninsula rely on the County’s soybean, corn and wheat crops as the primary 
ingredients in commercial chicken feed. 
 
Forest comprises the second-highest acreage of land use in the unincorporated area, totaling 
62,276.64 acres. The majority of this land use, about 30,000 acres, is deciduous forest; about 
25,000 acres are mixed deciduous and evergreen forest. Approximately one-third of the forests 
in the unincorporated area, about 22,000 acres, are forested wetlands.  
 
Residential land uses comprise 11,623.44 acres, approximately 6%, of the unincorporated area. 
Most of the residential development in the unincorporated area, 11,047 acres, is classified as low-
density and consists of single-family homes on large lots (e.g., a minimum of one acre). There 
are 523.75 acres of medium-density residential land use and 52.54 acres high-density residential 
land use in the unincorporated area. Residential land uses are permitted in the R-Rural (low and 
very low density), R-1 and R-2 Residential (medium density) VN Village Neighborhood (medium 
density) and VC Village Center (high density) zoning districts. 
 
Commercial land uses comprise 484.98 acres of the unincorporated area and include various 
retail, service and agricultural support businesses. Commercial uses are often associated with 
the historic character of the surrounding community and fulfill basic service needs for residents. 
These uses are permitted in the C-1 Commercial, C-2 Commercial and VC Village Center zoning 
districts. Home occupations are permitted in all zoning districts with certain conditions. Residential 
trucking businesses are permitted by Special Use Exception in the R-Rural zoning district. 
Commercial solar development is permitted by State mandate in all zoning districts. 
 
Industrial land uses comprise 404.22 acres of the unincorporated area. Industrial uses are 
permitted in the I-2 Light Industrial zoning district and generally include light manufacturing, 
fabricating, warehousing and wholesale distribution businesses that are compatible with adjacent 
land uses and have no or minimal adverse effects on public health and safety. 
 
Growth and Future Land Use 
 
The County’s Land Use element objectives are adjusted with each updated Comprehensive Plan 
to address current and projected economic, housing and demographic trends. Local, State and 
national trends impact Caroline County citizens and businesses and although the County’s 
primary land use goal historically has been to conserve agricultural land and natural resources, 
the objectives and strategies to achieve the goal are refined to address and adapt to changing 
trends over time.

<!-- page 36 -->
23 
 
The County’s primary land use goal to conserve agricultural and natural resources is achieved by 
directing higher density development to existing towns and population centers and protecting 
agriculture and natural resources from impacts from incompatible land uses. The County has 
used various tools to manage the location and intensity of development, including establishing a  
Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) program, designating tiered-density growth areas (by 2014 
State mandate), eliminating major residential subdivisions in the R-Rural Zone, creating a Priority 
Preservation Area (by State mandate) and establishing higher density development zoning 
districts in designated Rural Villages. Over time, zoning has remained the County’s most effective 
land use management tool to guide development in a manner that protects the County’s rural 
landscape and agriculture-based economy.  
 
County Growth Areas 
 
The Maryland Renewable Energy Certainty Act, adopted into State law in 2025, pre-empts local 
growth planning policy and zoning laws in order to facilitate large and utility-scale solar 
development to meet the State’s renewable energy goals. The new legislation forces jurisdictions 
to allow solar development in all zoning districts and areas designated for growth and severely 
limits local governments’ ability to protect rural and agricultural land and plan for future population 
needs. The pace and location of solar development is unknown until a project begins, which 
prevents jurisdictions from adequately planning for loss of land in zoning districts that were 
intended to accommodate agriculture or future growth and development. 
 
The new law requires County approval of solar developments in County-designated planned 
growth areas, including: 
 
- 
Tier I and Tier II septic growth areas (tiered residential growth areas mandated by the 
State in 2014) 
- 
Medium and high-density residential areas  
- 
Mixed–use areas with a residential component  
 
Although utility-scale solar development is prohibited in certain areas designated for residential 
growth, there is no limit to the number of smaller, community-scale solar projects that will be 
allowed in these areas, which will pose a significant threat to the acreage the County has 
designated to meet future housing needs. Growth areas are designated to support future 
population growth and economic development. County and municipal growth areas are 
established with potential build-out scenarios based on zoning density and are routinely assessed 
for capacity to serve projected increases in population.  
 
Although the County opposes large-scale commercial solar development because of the threat it 
poses to the County’s agricultural land and economy and its ability to meet future housing needs, 
the County wholeheartedly encourages the promotion of onsite individual solar systems that 
support the clean energy needs of residences, businesses and farms. The County is supportive 
of commercial solar development that utilizes space on rooftops and in the existing, built 
environment rather than on valuable farmland and land designated as optimal for meeting 
affordable housing needs.

<!-- page 37 -->
24 
 
 
County designated growth areas include the TDR Receiving Area and Rural Village Priority 
Funding Areas (PFAs). Because there is existing development in both the Receiving Area and 
Rural Villages, future growth would occur as infill development. Infill development is more 
economically feasible for developers because it avoids the high cost of building new roads and 
infrastructure and reduces building project timelines. The TDR Receiving Area’s location between 
the towns of Denton and Greensboro affords proximity to existing neighborhoods, infrastructure, 
schools, and commercial goods and services. Similarly, the Rural Village PFAs are existing 
population hubs with clustered housing and, in some cases, churches, parks and small-scale 
businesses and services. Because they are designated as Priority Funding Areas, Rural Villages 
are eligible for State funding for infrastructure development and improvements. 
 
The Rural Villages were designated as “Priority Funding Areas” under the State’s 1997 Smart 
Growth Areas Act. The PFA law was enacted to contain urban growth and focus state spending 
on existing communities and designated growth areas. Directing State funding for infrastructure 
like highways, sewer systems, and economic development aid to existing communities and locally 
designated growth areas encourages infill and revitalization while discouraging scattered 
development in rural, environmentally sensitive areas.  
 
The PFA program also ensures the orderly expansion of towns, which are municipal PFAs, and 
their infrastructure, while coordinating County and town land use and growth management goals 
to promote high quality development. County and municipal plans, policies, and regulations 
should coordinate the orderly and efficient transition of land in growth areas through municipal 
annexation and subsequent extension of public infrastructure and services. More discussion 
regarding the appropriate zoning classification for municipal growth areas will occur at the time of 
the County’s comprehensive rezoning.  
 
Rural Village Priority Funding Areas 
 
There are twelve Rural Village PFAs in Caroline County (see Map 2-* Rural Villages and 
Municipalities). As noted above, the Rural Village PFAs were established in 1997. 
 
The County established Rural Village zoning districts in 2012. These zoning districts are intended 
to provide for low to medium-density residential and commercial uses and can include land within 
the PFA boundary as well as adjoining land surrounding the boundary. Rural Village zoning 
districts include: 
 
VC, Village Center District: allows mixed uses of medium to high density commercial and 
residential development. The permitted commercial uses are similar to those allowed in the C-1 
Commercial District and are intended to serve the residents of the Rural Village and the 
surrounding area. The VC district is not designated in all PFA rural villages. Permitted density in 
the Village Center District is 8 lots/housing units per acre.

<!-- page 39 -->
26 
 
 
VN, Village Neighborhood District: allows medium-density residential use. Denser, smaller lot 
residential development is permitted in this district to maintain consistency with the historically 
denser development patterns of most of the County’s rural villages. These districts may eventually 
require public water and/or sewer service; however, services should not be the basis for new 
development inconsistent with the existing scale or character. Permitted density in the Village 
Neighborhood District is 4 lots/housing units per acre. 
 
The Rural Village zoning districts may offer increased opportunities for both higher density 
residential and commercial development to offset residential and commercial district losses to 
solar development. All Rural Villages are served by individual well and septic systems; the 
provision of water and sewer service will be necessary to accommodate denser development in  
the Village districts. The County will study the feasibility and timing of providing these services to 
meet future development needs.  All Rural Villages were originally designated Tier III septic 
growth areas; the County will redesignate the Rural Villages as Tier II septic growth areas planned 
for future sewer service. 
 
Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) Receiving Areas 
 
In 2006 with the revision of the County TDR Program, two receiving areas were officially 
designated which are essentially County Growth Areas. As shown on Map 2-4 Septic Growth 
Tiers, the larger receiving area consists of 3,386 acres located between Denton and Greensboro 
in a rural suburban area bisected by State Route 313 (Greensboro Road) and adjacent to the 
Choptank River. The second, smaller receiving area consists of 1,254 acres located across the 
Choptank River between Denton and Ridgely in a predominantly suburban area.    
 
Currently, 23.98% of the total TDR Receiving Areas are developed. In the past few years, the 
TDR Receiving Areas have been developing at a rate of less than 0.2% per year. 
 
It should be noted that farms that have leased or sold land for solar development are not eligible 
to participate in the TDR program.  
 
Septic Growth Tiers 
 
As noted above, the County designated its Septic Growth Tiers in 2014 (see Map 2-4 Septic 
Growth Tiers). The State developed four tiers of growth categories to identify where major and 
minor residential subdivisions may be located in counties and towns and what type of sewerage 
system (public sewerage or on-site septic) will serve them: 
 
• 
Tier I – areas currently served by sewerage systems. 
• 
Tier II – areas planned to be served by sewerage systems. 
• 
Tier III – areas not planned to be served by sewerage systems – these are areas where 
growth on septic systems can occur. Major subdivisions (greater than 4 lots) on individual 
on-site septic systems are only allowed to occur in Tier III. Community systems and 
individual on-site sewage disposal systems are permitted for residential major

<!-- page 40 -->
27 
 
subdivisions. This Tier includes the County’s TDR Receiving Area and designated Rural 
Villages. 
• 
Tier IV – areas planned for preservation and conservation where major residential 
subdivisions are prohibited. Only minor subdivisions (generally 4 lots) are allowed in Tier 
IV.  
 
Although the new law prohibits utility-scale solar development in Tier 1 and Tier II areas, which 
include municipal growth areas, the encroachment of community-scale solar development, which  
is allowed in those areas, will pose a threat to land that is prioritized by the County and its 
municipalities for future growth needs.  
 
Utility-scale solar development is permitted in Tier III, which includes PFA Rural Villages and the 
TDR Receiving Area – both of which the County has designated for future growth and 
development.  
 
The County will review its Tier designations to ensure adequate opportunities exist for residential, 
commercial and industrial development as encroachment by solar development reduces those 
opportunities around the County. 
 
Existing Municipalities 
 
The County’s ten municipalities (see Map 2-* Rural Villages and Municipalities) contain its highest 
concentration of residential density and economic activity. Directing growth to municipalities is the 
most efficient way to reduce pressure for development in rural areas and ensure that future growth 
is supported by adequate infrastructure and services. Consequently, the municipalities play an 
important role in the County’s growth management strategies.  
 
As established population centers, the towns are the preferred location for future population 
growth and non-agricultural economic activity in the region, in accordance with State Smart 
Growth laws. Continued cooperation between the County and Towns will build the community 
resources necessary to effectively implement growth management and revitalization strategies 
and achieve economies of scale, while also preserving jurisdictional integrity. 
 
Accommodating Growth 2025 - 2045 
 
In 2025, the total County population was 34,230; Maryland Department of Planning (MDP) 
projects the County’s total population will reach 37,160 by 2040, an overall increase of 12%. MDP 
projects the County’s average annual growth rate will remain steady at .6% before decreasing 
slightly to .5% by 2040. The slight decline in growth rate is projected based on the aging 
population and a resulting general decrease in natural population increase (births minus deaths).

•
