The Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE) issues ground water appropriation and use 
permits that allocate average and maximum daily flow capacities for public and community water 
systems. Annual average daily flow is the total volume of water flowing into a water facility during 
any consecutive 365 days, divided by 365 and expressed in units of mgd (million gallons per day) 
or gpd (gallons per day). Maximum daily flow capacity is the maximum quantity permitted to flow 
within a single 24-hour period. 
Six of the County’s ten municipalities own and operate public water systems: Denton, 
Federalsburg, Greensboro, Henderson, Preston and Ridgely. 
 
Town of Denton 
 
The Town of Denton water system is comprised of three groundwater wells that draw from the 
Piney Point Aquifer. The system has a permit for 770,000 gpd average annual use, and a 
maximum permitted use of 1,000,000 gpd. 
 
According to the Town’s 2023 Comprehensive Plan, in 2017, 2018, and 2019 (3-year average), 
the average daily demand for Denton's water system was 317,250 gallons per day, approximately 
51% of the system's permitted daily capacity. The Town has accessed its water and wastewater 
systems and concluded that both have functional and permitted capacities to support projected 
growth through 2040 (the Comprehensive Plan planning period).  
 
Town of Federalsburg 
 
The Town of Federalsburg water system draws water from five wells; two draw from the 
Federalsburg Aquifer and three draw from the Cheswold Aquifer. In 2007, MDE issued the Town 
two water appropriation permits totaling 600,000 gpd annual average use and 760,000 gpd 
maximum use. 
 
In 2006, the five-year average daily use of water in the Town was 401,706 gpd, approximately 40 
percent of the system’s average daily capacity.

<!-- page 79 -->
66 
 
Federalsburg’s Comprehensive Plan Water Resources Element, adopted in 2009, projects only 
a minor increase in water withdrawals in the Town as a result of growth, with no significant impact 
expected on the region’s groundwater supply. 
 
Town of Greensboro 
 
Greensboro withdraws its water from three wells in the Piney Point Aquifer. In 2008, the Town’s 
MDE Appropriation and Use Permit was increased from 200,000 gpd average annual use to 
325,000 gpd and from 300,000 gpd maximum use to 455,000 gpd. 
 
According to the Town’s most recent Comprehensive Plan, in 2007 the average daily demand on 
Greensboro’s water system was 183,551 gpd, about 57 percent of its permitted average daily 
capacity. The five-year average daily use was 183,000 gpd; approximately 55 percent of the 
system’s permitted average daily capacity. The Town anticipates that growth in the form of infill 
development will not deplete the system’s capacity, but growth beyond existing Town boundaries 
will require an increase in plant capacity and possible improvements in pumping and storage 
capabilities. 
 
Town of Henderson 
 
The Town of Henderson utilizes three wells to withdraw water from the Piney Point Aquifer. 
Henderson is permitted to withdraw an average of 15,000 gpd annually, 20,000 gpd maximum. 
 
Town of Preston 
 
The Town of Preston’s water system is comprised of two wells that draw water from the Piney 
Point Aquifer at depths of 600 and 533 feet. An elevated tank provides 150,000 gallons of storage; 
chlorination is provided at the Town’s water treatment plant. The system is permitted to withdraw 
an annual average of 80,000 gpd and a maximum of 120,000 gpd. In 2024 the system’s average 
daily flow was 66,240 gpd. 
 
The Town’s most recent Comprehensive Plan Water Resources Element assumes a modest 
development scenario of 72 additional dwelling units over a twenty-year planning period. The 
Town’s current available capacity of 15,085 gpd represents a maximum development potential of 
42 additional dwellings; to accommodate additional growth an additional well and new water 
appropriation permit would be necessary. The Town will closely monitor water usage and begin 
procedures to increase the existing permits to prevent delays with infill development and future 
projects. 
 
Town of Ridgely 
 
The Ridgely water system is comprised of two wells that draw from the Piney Point aquifer. The 
Town’s existing water tower has a capacity of 200,000 gallons per day (gpd). In 2026 the system

<!-- page 80 -->
67 
 
had an average daily flow of 118,000 gpd.  
 
The Town is planning to construct an additional water tower with a capacity of 300,000 gpd to 
ensure sufficient water storage volume for fire protection, adequate back-up and emergency shut 
down reserve and equalization by well pumps (required storage tank volumes are calculated by 
combining these three elements of water demand).  An additional well and new treatment facility 
are also planned for the Town’s water system. The addition of one new well will serve the planned 
new water tower and as a back-up for the current wells and help meet the overtaxed current 
system demand. The new treatment plant will be constructed with communication controls, 
provide higher treatment capacity and a higher treatment quality, and tie-in to the newly proposed 
well. A new water main will be constructed to connect the new water system components into the 
Town’s existing water system.  The system’s GAP permit will be amended to accommodate the 
additional new well and higher withdrawal capacity. 
 
The Town began the application process with MDE in 2023 for the planned improvements and 
will proceed with the project pending approval of funding.  
 
Small Community/Multi-User Water Systems 
 
Small community or multi-user water systems in Caroline County include systems that serve small 
areas or multiple users, such as major subdivisions, industrial facilities, school campuses, and 
mobile home parks. Public schools in the County that operate small systems include Colonel 
Richardson High School (in the southern half of the County near American Corner) and North 
Caroline High School, located east of Denton. The Caroline County Board of Education owns 
these systems and contracts licensed operators to manage them. 
 
Most of the County’s small systems withdraw water from the Piney Point Aquifer at permitted rates 
of less than 10,000 gpd.23 In 2003 Caroline Acres and Cedar mobile home parks were two of the 
largest users at 73,000 gpd and 35,000 gpd, respectively.

Caroline County Future Water Use and Demand 
 
Residential 
 
Increases in Caroline County’s residential water use can be approximately predicted based on 
Maryland Department of Planning projections for population and households. To calculate 
residential water usage, a coefficient of 250 gpd per household (MDE estimate of per dwelling 
unit daily water usage) was applied to the number of households projected in the County. 
Projections for future residential water usage indicate a total increase of approximately 17 percent 
between 2025 and 2055. The average 5-year incremental increase during this period is 3%.

Based on the modest amount of population growth projected by the Maryland Department of 
Planning for Caroline County, and the Maryland Department of Environment’s confirmation that 
water resource indicators for Maryland indicate that there is an abundance of water to meet the 
State’s present and future needs, the County projects that available water resources will be 
sufficient to meet future demand. 
. 
Agricultural 
 
The limitations of available data make future agricultural water usage less predictable. In 2016, 
Maryland Department of Environment (MDE) commissioned “Water Use in Maryland - A Workplan 
to Advance Water Use Science”,24 which includes strategies for improving water use data 
management and improving the accuracy of Maryland’s water use information. The workplan notes 
that “The quality and reliability of monthly irrigation reporting data is uncertain, due to the use of 
indirect units (e.g. pumping hours) and specific knowledge of the crops being grown on the farm for 
 
24 Water Use in Maryland - A Workplan to Advance Water Use Science 2017-2020, Prepared for the 
Maryland Department of the Environment by Stuart S. Schwartz, PhD., Center for Urban Environmental 
Research and Education, University of Maryland Baltimore County, 2016.

<!-- page 82 -->
69 
 
the year in question. Reported data may not always be sufficient to gauge compliance. Metering of 
agricultural irrigation is not required by Maryland, and meters introduce their own idiosyncratic 
problems with systematic underestimation and overestimation reported as the impellor can be 
driven by positive and negative pressure surges as systems drain down, and well levels recover.” 
The 2016 workplan lists irrigation water use as a high priority data need.  
 
Caroline County is committed to preserving agriculture as an economic, historic, cultural and 
scenic resource and understands the critical need to preserve the natural resources that sustain 
agriculture. Regional planning and management of water resources must include ensuring future 
agricultural water needs along with future water demand as a result of growth. Caroline County 
supports State and federal efforts to assist farmers with developing cost-effective irrigation 
systems that combine technology and best management practices to conserve valuable water 
resources. The County supports the development of irrigation systems that aid in controlling non-
point source pollution from agricultural lands by enabling crops to maximize nutrient uptake during 
dry or drought years.

Until the NACP aquifer study is complete, the most recent technical information available about 
Caroline County’s major aquifers indicates that the County’s demand for groundwater is not 
currently significantly impacting the yields of its major aquifers. Further, the 1998 study performed 
on the Columbia and Piney Point aquifers in Caroline County indicates that increased usage as a 
result of projected growth and increased demand for irrigation in the County will not be detrimental 
to the aquifers’ yields in the foreseeable future. The more likely threat to the sustainability of the 
County’s major aquifers will come from significant increases in usages of the same aquifers in 
other areas of the State and in Delaware. 
Projected long-term water demand in Caroline County will need to be measured against aquifer 
sustainability estimates developed as part of the continuing NACP aquifer study. As the NACP 
study continues and a more comprehensive analysis of the region’s water supply is developed,

<!-- page 83 -->
70 
 
the County (along with the State) will have an improved ability to make informed decisions 
regarding allocation of water resources for specific uses. Water allocation policies should include 
ensuring adequate future supply for agricultural users along with other major users. 
The County will use the information systems developed by the MDE to monitor trends in County 
water usage supplies and to compare new data with projections of County water demand. 
Analysis of this data will enable the County to anticipate and address critical issues before they 
arise. 
Caroline County will coordinate planning strategies with state and local governments to ensure 
that regional needs are accounted for in water resources planning at the local level. The 
County recommends that MDE’s Water Rights Division improve its coordination of water 
permitting and planning with County and municipal governments to ensure that State policies and 
practices do not conflict or undo local efforts to conserve and enhance local and regional water 
supplies.  
 
Regular assessments of regional water supplies – and capacity issues – should be conducted by 
MDE to afford local governments and citizens a better understanding of the sustainability of the 
County’s – and the region’s – water resources. 
 
In the event the results of the NACP study indicate that any of Caroline County’s major aquifers 
are endangered, the County should take immediate action to protect existing water sources and 
identify alternative water sources. Depending on the availability of alternative water sources, the 
County may consider petitioning EPA to designate critical water source as a Sole Source Aquifer. 
 
The Sole Source Aquifer (SSA) Program, which is authorized by Section 1424(e) of the Safe 
Drinking Water Act, allows communities to petition the EPA for protection when a community is 
dependent on a single source of drinking water and there is no possibility of a replacement water 
supply to be found. EPA defines a sole or principal source aquifer as one which supplies at least 
50% of the drinking water consumed in the area overlying the aquifer. EPA guidelines also 
stipulate that these areas can have no alternative drinking water source(s) which would 
physically, legally, and economically supply all those who depend upon the aquifer for drinking 
water. Petitions for SSA designation are submitted to EPA along with usage data and other 
technical and administrative information. EPA regional offices review petitions and, if merited, the 
Regional Administrator will designate an area as a Sole Source Aquifer. 
 
Water Supply Implementation 
 
• 
Review development applications and requests for County Water and Sewer Plan 
amendments for: 
- 
Large water usage impacts on water supply (domestic well and municipal systems); 
- 
Impairment risks based on proximity of development to wells and wellhead protection 
areas.

<!-- page 84 -->
71 
 
 
• 
Coordinate with Maryland Department of Environment to address potential impacts from 
large water users and potential ground and surface water impacts from development. 
 
• 
Review annual MDE/USGS surface and groundwater withdrawal and capacity data when 
it becomes available to establish trends in usage and determine if actions is needed to 
address: 
- 
Potential supply or water quality impacts to surficial aquifer users 
- 
Potential drought-related impacts to baseline stream flow 
 
• 
Coordinate with Maryland Department of Environment to address data trends that indicate 
increasing pressure on water resources. 
 
 
Part 2: Water Quality 
 
Water Quality Laws, Initiatives and Programs 
 
Federal Clean Water Act 
 
The Federal Clean Water Act (CWA), enacted in 1972, provides the framework for managing the 
nation’s water resources. It is the primary federal law for protecting waterways from pollution. The 
law enacts water quality standards “to restore and maintain the chemical, physical, and biological 
integrity of the Nation’s waters” (Clean Water Act Section 101). The standards include designated 
uses for waterways as well as specific criteria that indicate whether or not the uses are able to 
be achieved in each waterway.  
 
Designated uses are identified through a public process and are based on the use and value of 
the water body for: 
 
1) public water supply;  
2) protection of fish, shellfish, and wildlife; and/or  
3) recreational, agricultural, industrial, and navigational purposes (including swimming, fishing 
and boating).  
 
These designated uses provide the foundation for determining which of Maryland’s waterways 
are managed under the CWA. Once a waterway’s designated use (or uses) has been established, 
stringent water quality criteria are developed to ensure the protection of the designated use. Water 
quality criteria identify quantifiable pollutant limits that are not to be exceeded. A waterway is 
identified as impaired when it no longer meets the water quality criteria established for it and it is 
unable to achieve the use for which it is designated.

<!-- page 85 -->
72 
 
States are required to conduct a scientific study to determine the maximum load of a specific 
pollutant a waterbody can receive while still meeting water quality standards. This limit, or Total 
Maximum Daily Load (TMDL), serves as a "pollution budget" for impaired waterbodies that do not 
meet water quality standards. While federal law sets the requirement to create TMDLs, it does 
not prescribe or mandate specific procedures for implementing them. This leaves the 
implementation planning largely to states, often leading to varied approaches. 
 
In Maryland, legal responsibility for water quality is a shared effort between the state and local 
governments. Although MDE sets water quality standards, authority is delegated to local 
governments to inspect and enforce critical regulations related to development, such as sediment 
and erosion control. Local jurisdictions are also responsible for managing stormwater through 
Environmental Site Design (ESD) techniques to reduce pollution and flooding for projects 
disturbing 5,000 square feet or more of land. 
 
EPA Clean Water Act Section 319 grants provide money to states to help communities reduce 
water pollution caused by runoff. In 2010, in partnership the MDE 319(h) Program, the County 
completed the Upper Choptank River Watershed 319(h) Watershed Plan, which includes 
strategies for non-point source pollution reduction throughout the Upper Choptank River 
watershed, the County’s largest watershed. EPA approved and certified the Plan on December 
20, 2010. In mid-2011, the County completed its analyses of nutrient loadings for all 70 of its 
HUC-12 watersheds. These analyses were used to identify and prioritize locations where BMP 
implementation projects offer the most opportunity for nutrient reduction and water quality 
improvement. In late 2011, the County partnered with the Town of Denton and the Town of 
Greensboro to complete more in-depth analyses of the HUC-12 watershed located in each town 
and to develop implementation strategies for those watersheds. These strategies include site 
assessments for urban and rural stormwater management best management practices, 
preliminary design concepts, and budget estimates for labor and materials. 
 
The Plan serves as the foundation of the County’s water quality improvement goals and has 
furthered partnerships with the University of Maryland, the Center for Watershed Protection, 
Shore Rivers/Choptank Riverkeeper, the Chesapeake Bay Foundation, the Eastern Shore Land 
Conservancy, and other key stakeholders.  
 
Chesapeake Bay TMDL 
 
On December 29, 2010, the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) established the 
Chesapeake Bay Total Maximum Daily Load (Bay TMDL) for nitrogen, phosphorus and sediment 
from various sources such as agricultural operations, urban and suburban stormwater and 
wastewater. The Bay TMDL was developed by the EPA in conjunction with Bay jurisdictions 
(Delaware, Maryland, New York, Pennsylvania, Virginia, West Virginia, and the District of 
Columbia) and was the largest ever developed by EPA, encompassing the Bay’s entire 64,000-
square-mile watershed.

<!-- page 86 -->
73 
 
The Chesapeake Bay and its tributaries contain excess nutrients and sediment from various 
sources (“sectors”) such as agricultural operations, urban and suburban stormwater, wastewater, 
and air pollution. The excess nutrients and sediment lead to murky water and algae blooms, which 
block sunlight from reaching and sustaining underwater Bay grasses, and create low levels of 
dissolved oxygen, which is critical for aquatic life such as fish, crabs, and oysters. 
 
The TMDL established Maryland’s wasteload allocations by local jurisdiction and major basin, 
and within those boundaries by pollution source (agriculture and developed land nonpoint 
sources, septics, and wastewater and urban stormwater permits). 
 
Caroline County’s major tributaries – the Choptank River, Marshyhope Creek and Tuckahoe 
Creek – are all included in the Chesapeake Bay TMDL for nutrients and sediment. MDE 
established a TMDL for sediment specifically for the Upper Choptank River in 2019. An additional 
impairment was listed in the Maryland 2024 IR due to PFOs (Perfluorooctane Sulfonate, a ‘forever 
chemical’) found in fish tissue in the Choptank River (a TMDL for phosphorus was specifically 
established for Marshyhope Creek in 2000 through the CWA). 
 
In 2012, local jurisdictions including Caroline County submitted Watershed Implementation Plans 
(WIPs) designed to strengthen the initial cleanup strategies and reflect the involvement of local 
governments and partners. Maryland incorporated this plan into the State’s Phase I WIP. The 
State WIP has been revised twice (Phase II and Phase III WIPs), which update to reflect improved 
water quality monitoring and progress, and to outline refined actions to meet remaining goals. Bay 
jurisdictions also are required to submit two-year milestones and progress data outlining near-
term restoration commitments and achievements of previous milestones. Milestones generally 
include: enhancements to existing water quality programs; establishment of new programs or 
initiatives; capacity building and implementation actions; development or improvement of tools or 
datasets; and adoption of modified or new regulations, legislation, or policies. EPA reviews 
milestones and progress and works with states to identify gaps and determine if regulatory actions 
are necessary for progress. 
 
The Maryland Phase III WIP heavily relies on the State’s two leading sectors of nutrient and 
sediment pollution, agricultural (52%) and wastewater (42%), to achieve reductions, along with 
storm water management. 
 
Tier II Waters 
 
Tier II waters are high-quality water bodies (streams, rivers, lakes) designated by the Maryland 
Department of the Environment based on exceptional water quality, habitat, and biological 
communities. The State’s water quality standards include Designated Uses, the criteria to protect 
Uses, and an Antidegradation Policy. The Antidegradation policy protects waters where water 
quality exceeds the minimum requirements specified by the State, including Tier II waters. 
Implementation procedures were developed for Tier II waters to protect and maintain them as 
high quality waters so they are not allowed to degrade to meet only the minimum standards (Tier

<!-- page 87 -->
74 
 
I). Apart from certain short-term changes, water quality cannot be lowered in Tier II waters. 
 
MDE recommends stream segments for Tier II consideration after measuring and monitoring data 
against biological criteria and water quality thresholds. If a segment exceeds minimum water 
quality standards, it is eligible for Tier II consideration. If a segment is designated Tier II, any new 
or proposed amendments to County water and sewer plans and NDPES discharge permits are 
required to assure consistency with anti-degradation requirements. A Maryland Tier II stream 
catchment basin is a specifically designated watershed area that drains into a Tier II stream. 
 
Caroline County has twelve designated Tier II stream segments; the streams and their catchment 
basins are illustrated on the Tier II Waters map. 
       
Bay Restoration Fund Enhanced Nutrient Reduction (ENR) 
 
The Bay Restoration Fund (BRF) was created by Senate Bill 246 in May, 2004. The BRF uses 
funding from public sewer taxes and individual septic system fees to provide up to 100 percent 
state grant funds to local governments to retrofit or upgrade sewage treatment plants to reduce 
the nutrient levels in plant discharge to Enhanced Nutrient Removal (ENR) levels: 3 mg/l total 
nitrogen (TN) and .3 mg/l total phosphorus (TP). Upon completion of an ENR upgrade, MDE 
requires the permittee to make a best effort to meet the load goals, providing reasonable 
assurance of implementation. 
 
The BRF also funds the cost of connecting properties served by septic systems to ENR treatment 
plants and installing denitrification upgrades for septic systems. Denitrification systems remove 
50 percent or more of the nitrogen discharged by septic systems. The Chesapeake Bay Nitrogen 
Reduction Act, passed at the end of the State’s last legislative session, requires that septic 
systems being built or replaced for homes located within the Critical Area must utilize the "best 
available technology" to reduce the level of nitrogen output of the septic system. The Caroline 
County office of Maryland Department of Environmental Health oversees implementation of the 
BRF program and administration of the law and fund. 
 
The Maryland Water Quality Improvement Act 
 
The Maryland Water Quality Improvement Act “requires that comprehensive and enforceable 
nutrient management plans be developed, approved and implemented for all agricultural lands 
throughout Maryland.” This act specifically requires that farm nutrient management plans for 
nitrogen be developed and implemented by 2002, and plans for phosphorus to be done by 2005. 
In 2008, 379 farming operations filed nutrient management plans with MDA; however only 80 
percent, about 90,000 acres, reported that their nutrient management plans were being 
implemented. EPA, through the Chesapeake Bay Program, continues to emphasize that 
achieving 100 percent implementation of agricultural nutrient management plans is critical to 
achieving nutrient reduction. Maryland Department of Agriculture, with assistance from the Soil 
Conservation District and USDA Farm Service Agency, are responsible for assisting farmers with

<!-- page 88 -->
75 
 
the preparation and implementation of nutrient management plans and oversee annual 
compliance checks statewide. 
 
Caroline County Water Quality 
 
Watersheds 
     
Watersheds are classified by the U.S. Geological Survey (USGS) by Hydrologic Unit Code (HUC). 
HUC watersheds range from 2 to 12 digits, representing 6 levels of drainage area size, from large 
regions (2-digit) to small sub-watersheds (12-digit).  
 
Caroline County is located within six designated 8-digit watersheds: Upper Choptank River, 
Tuckahoe Creek, Marshyhope Creek, Lower Choptank River, Nanticoke River, and Upper     
Chester River. Over half (58%) of the County is located in the Upper Choptank Watershed; 
together, the Upper Choptank River, Tuckahoe Creek and Marshyhope Creek watersheds occupy 
nearly the entire County.

In 2010 the County developed the 2010 Upper Choptank River 319(h) Watershed Plan to identify 
opportunities to reduce nutrient and sediment nonpoint source pollution in County’s portion of the 
Upper Choptank River watershed, which is the largest watershed in the County. The plan was 
developed jointly by MDE and Caroline County (Department of Planning and Codes and Soil 
Conservation District) with assistance from the Center for Watershed Protection and includes 
goals and strategies for reducing nutrient and sediment impacts from nonpoint pollution sources 
(agricultural and developed land and septic systems). Projects that implement Plan goals are 
funded by MDE’s 319(h) nonpoint source grant program. To date the County has implemented 
sixteen projects on developed lands with funding provided by the MDE 319(h) grant program that 
have collectively reduced hundreds of pounds of nutrient and sediment impacts to the Choptank 
River from stormwater runoff and septic systems.

assistance from the MDE 319 grant program and watershed partners, including University of 
Maryland Sea Grant Extension, the Choptank Riverkeeper, Center for Watershed Protection and 
the Envision the Choptank partnership. 
 
Point and Non-point Source Pollution 
 
Point and non-point source pollution are the two primary ways contaminants enter waterways. 
Point source pollution includes measurable inputs of pollutants discharged into streams, rivers 
and lakes via pipes or drains, such as wastewater treatment facilities and stormwater drains. Non-
point source pollution comes from many different places, often carried over land by water via 
stormwater or snowmelt. Because there is no specific location where this runoff enters waterways 
(i.e., no pipe) is harder to identify, trace, and regulate. Septic systems deliver nitrogen impacts 
below the surface via subsurface septic drainfields and are generally included in the non-point 
source category. 
 
Point Source Pollution 
 
The National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (NPDES) program, created by the 1972 
CWA, is a federal program that regulates point sources that discharge pollutants into waters of 
the United States. It requires facilities to obtain permits restricting wastewater discharge to meet 
water quality standards. In Maryland, the National Pollution Discharge Elimination System 
(NPDES) program is administered by MDE’s Water Management Administration. The program 
requires permits for discharges of more than 10,000 gallons of water a day within a one-month 
period, or any discharge of waste to surface or groundwater. MDE reviews and renews NPDES 
permits every five years. 
 
Wastewater treatment plants (WWTPs) require NPDES permits to discharge treated sewage into 
surface water or the ground. Permitted facilities must adhere to water quality standards as well 
as effluent limits. An effluent limit is a condition of a discharge permit which limits the amount of a 
particular pollutant that may be discharged into the water body. Agricultural activities which may 
require an NPDES permit include animal waste facilities, aquaculture operations, crop irrigation, 
and large concentrated animal feeding operations. The NPDES permit includes restrictions and 
monitoring requirements that ensure protection of water quality and aquatic life.  
 
Point source pollution reductions are managed under the jurisdiction of the permitting authority 
(MDE) and the local governments and private entities that own treatment plants. Caroline County 
does not own or operate any industrial or public wastewater treatment facilities. The public 
wastewater treatment facilities located in the County are owned and operated by the towns of 
Denton, Federalsburg, Greensboro, Preston, and Ridgely. Small industrial and community sewer 
systems in Caroline County include schools, industrial and commercial sites and mobile home 
parks.  
 
MDE’s Water Management Administration oversees compliance requirements and enforcement

<!-- page 91 -->
78 
 
of industrial and wastewater treatment facility NPDES permits. Common issues experienced at 
these facilities include aging equipment, excessive flows, and groundwater contamination. 
System owners work with MDE’s compliance and enforcement divisions to correct permit 
violations, maintain system efficiencies, and enhance treatment technology when advisable. 
 
Non-Point Source Nutrient Loads 
 
The majority of nutrient and sediment loads entering waterways in Caroline County are from non-
point sources.  Non-point source pollution occurs when rainfall, snowmelt, or irrigation runs off 
land or through the ground and gathers pollutants, which are carried with the runoff and deposited 
into surface water or leaked into ground water. The amount of stormwater runoff in developed 
areas is a function of the amount of impervious surface associated with the built environment, i.e., 
roads, parking areas, roofs, etc. The greater the percentage of impervious surface, the faster 
water flows over land. In wooded or heavily vegetated areas, the water is intercepted by 
undergrowth, plants and trees as it flows over land. Runoff in these areas reaches streams more 
gradually, a process that underscores the importance of grass and forest streamside buffers, 
particularly on agricultural land. These natural impediments reduce flood-related pollution 
discharges and enable lower, slower flows which in turn reduce the potential for erosion caused 
by storm events. The slower pace of runoff from undeveloped land also allows time for vegetation 
to uptake the nutrients in the runoff, which results in lower nutrient loads being discharged into 
waterways. 
 
Agricultural Non-Point Pollution Sources 
 
Agricultural land, which comprises more than half (59 percent) of the County’s total land area, 
delivers the largest non-point source loads in the County via nutrients from fertilizer, manure and 
sediment in runoff from farm fields.  
 
The Maryland Department of Agriculture (MDA) is overseeing the statewide-effort to reduce non-
point source pollution from agricultural land. MDA has developed a list of agricultural best 
management practices (BMPs) to help Maryland meet its milestone commitments to meet the Bay 
cleanup goals. MDA along with its conservation partners provides a number of financial and 
technical resources to the agricultural community to adopt conservation practices. The Maryland 
Agricultural Water Quality Cost-Share Program (MACS) is the primary state program that provides 
farmers with grants to help cover the cost to install BMPs on their farms to control soil erosion, 
manage nutrients and safeguard water quality. 
 
Caroline County is supporting the MDA effort through implementation of voluntary stormwater 
projects on rural and agricultural land via grant-funded stormwater management projects 
coordinated with partners including University of Maryland Sea Grant Extension, the Center for 
Watershed Protection and the Envision the Choptank partnership.  
 
Developed Land Non-Point Pollution Sources

<!-- page 92 -->
79 
 
 
Developed land, which includes residential, commercial, institutional and industrial buildings and 
their surrounding built environments, comprises about 7 percent of the County. Hard surfaces 
such as rooftops, pavement and compacted dirt and gravel areas add speed and power to the 
flow of stormwater runoff, to the degree that large, highly developed urban areas are required to 
obtain NPDES stormwater permits to treat their associated high stormwater runoff flows. Caroline 
County has no large areas of highly developed land, even within municipalities, and consequently 
there are NPDES stormwater permit requirements in Caroline County. 
 
Septic System Pollution Sources 
 
Nitrogen loads from on-site septic systems are also included in the State’s assessment of the 
County’s total non-point source load. Nearly all properties located in the unincorporated areas of 
the County and some properties located within municipal boundaries are served by septic 
systems, approximately 12,000 in 2025. About 13 percent (approximately 1,500) of septic 
systems in the County are located within the Critical Area, i.e., within 1,000 feet of tidal waters. 
 
Septic systems are used to treat and discharge wastewater from toilets, sinks, bathtubs, 
dishwashers, washing machines, and other water-consumptive items. To work effectively, a septic 
system requires proper siting and installation and regular maintenance. A failing septic system is 
one that discharges effluent with pollutant concentrations that exceed established water quality 
standards. A report published in 2000 by the Center for Watershed Protection cites research that 
indicates that typical failure rates for septic systems range from one to five percent each year.  
Improperly functioning septic systems are recognized as significant contributors of nitrogen loads 
to ground and surface water, particularly in rural areas where most property owners are served 
by individual septic systems. MDE estimates that septic systems discharge 9.5 pounds of nitrogen 
per person, per household, per year. The proximity of the system to a waterway is a major factor 
in what percentage of the nitrogen reaches a nearby waterway. MDE calculates a transport factor 
to determine the percentage of nitrogen that reaches a nearby waterway from a septic drainfield. 
The transport factor reflect the percentage of nitrogen that is absorbed through uptake in plants 
and trees before it is eventually discharged into a waterway. The longer the distance nitrogen 
must travel before it reaches a waterway, the more nitrogen is able to be absorbed along the path.  
MDE calculates that 80 percent of the nitrogen from systems located within 1,000 feet of tidal 
waters (i.e., the Critical Area) reaches the tidal waterway. Fifty percent of the nitrogen from 
systems located within 1,000 feet of a stream reaches a stream; thirty percent of the nitrogen from 
all other systems reaches a waterway.  
 
In 2008 the Maryland Board of Public Works voted to approve more than $6.6 million in Bay 
Restoration Funds (BRF) to upgrade septic systems with best available technology (BAT) for 
denitrification to prevent excess nitrogen from discharging to the State’s waterways. The nitrogen 
load from a BAT system is 50 to 80 percent less than the load from a conventional system. Seven 
counties, including Caroline County, receive $277,000 to $1.9 million each to upgrade septic 
systems with nutrient removing (denitrification) technology (Caroline County’s share of the Fund

<!-- page 93 -->
80 
 
is currently approximately $400,000).  
 
The Caroline County office of Maryland Environmental Health Services (EHS) oversees the BRF 
program to install denitrification septic systems in Caroline County. At present, one Registered 
Sanitarian (RS) and the EHS Director manage the program, including notifying property owners 
of available BRF funding, reviewing system specifications and requirements for candidate 
properties, and prioritizing eligible properties for funding. Failing systems located within the 
Critical Area are given top priority for BRF funds. 
 
During the development of the Phase III WIP, the State coordinated planning efforts with local 
government staff and nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) to develop strategies for meeting 
2025 Bay TMDL targets for nitrogen, phosphorus and sediment reductions in each sector in each 
county. The sectors include agriculture, developed land, septic, and wastewater. In its 2023 
review of Maryland’s progress for the Phase III WIP, EPA identified achievements in each sector 
as well as areas where improvements are needed. According to the data provided for the 2023 
progress review, Maryland achieved its statewide 2023 targets for phosphorus and sediment but 
not for nitrogen. Because there is still a gap in meeting the nitrogen goal, the State’s Phase III 
WIP focus is on meeting nitrogen reduction targets. 
 
Maryland’s nitrogen reduction goals for counties include feasible targets based on the availability 
of State assistance and adequate resources, including funding and staff support at optimal levels 
to incentivize increased restoration efforts and maintain existing pollution abatement practices 
and strategies.25 The State goals Caroline County nitrogen reductions are summarized below.

Non-Point Source Load Reductions 
 
Agricultural Land Nitrogen Reductions 
 
Maryland Department of Agriculture (MDA), which is managing statewide TMDL strategies for the 
agriculture sector, worked with county Soil Conservation Districts (SCDs) to establish county 
Phase III WIP goals for agricultural non-point source nitrogen loads. The goals rely on continuing 
resources and support to maintain a high pace of agricultural best management practices (BMPs) 
implementation and verification that is needed to ensure this sector will meet its goals. MDA is 
coordinating with state and federal funding agencies to ensure that cost-sharing and other funding 
programs maximize the application of resources toward achieving the state’s Phase III WIP 
nitrogen targets for agriculture. 
 
BMPs are designed to reduce nutrient loads from specific types of land. BMPs in Maryland, such 
as cover crops, nutrient management, and stream buffers, are designed to significantly reduce 
nitrogen, phosphorus, and sediment runoff into the Bay and its tributaries. Agricultural BMPs 
including installation of forest and grass buffers, implementation of soil conservation, water quality 
and nutrient management plans, planting of cover crops and buffers, and installation of drainage 
water control systems have been implemented on hundreds of Caroline County farms since the 
1990s.

<!-- page 95 -->
82 
 
 
Maryland Agricultural Water Quality Cost-Share Program (MACS) grants support farmers’ 
installation of BMPs on their farms that meet water quality criteria for controlling soil loss or animal 
waste. Any one or combination of MACS adopted practices allows a farm operation to address or 
prevent control of agriculturally related nonpoint water pollution specific to that operation’s needs. 
The MACS program contributes up to 87.5 percent of the eligible cost to implement practices; the 
farm operation contributes up to 12.5 percent. The farmer determines the BMPs and the locations 
of BMPs to be implemented. 
 
NRCS is focusing programmatic resources available to Maryland farmers, providing prioritized 
funding to the implementation of practices that were identified as most cost effective in meeting 
county reduction goals. This effort continues through the Environmental Quality Incentive 
Program (EQIP) and the Chesapeake Bay Watershed Initiative. The MACS Program leverages 
State funding by cost sharing the implementation of individual BMPs funded through these federal 
programs. 
 
In Caroline County, MDA is working with SCD staff to track voluntary farm practices to implement 
best management practices through MACS/NRCS programs including grass and forest buffers, 
cover crops, nutrient management plans and conservation tillage methods. Maryland approved 
low-till and no-till practices that focus on minimizing soil disturbance, reducing erosion, and 
enhancing nutrient management by maintaining surface residue, largely guided by USDA-NRCS 
standards, which require that farmers maintain 15 percent or greater crop residue coverage 
immediately after planting for tillage to be considered a BMP. 
 
According to the 2022 USDA Census of Agriculture, 48 percent of Caroline County farmers utilized 
no till BMPs, 20 percent utilized reduced till BMPs, and 13 percent utilized conventional intensive 
till practices (non-BMP). Approximately 26 percent of Caroline County farmers were enrolled in 
the cover crop BMP program.  
 
The Conservation Reserve Program, or CRP, was authorized by Congress in the 1985 Farm Bill, 
and is reauthorized in subsequent farm bills. The CRP is managed by the Department of 
Agriculture’s Farm Service Agency and provides funding and technical support to farmers who 
agree to take environmentally sensitive land out of crop production and instead plant permanent 
vegetation. 
 
As of January 2024, the total of 1,600 acres of conservation practices have been installed on 
Caroline County farms through the CRP program. Practices installed include filter strips, 
streamside buffers, upland habitat buffers, wetland restoration, grass plantings and grass 
waterways.  
 
As part of the Phase III implementation effort, MDA also recognizes the importance of 
documenting the efforts of the agricultural community to install practices without the technical or 
financial assistance of the department and its partners. Through its Resource Improvements

<!-- page 96 -->
83 
 
process, MDA is using a certified process developed in 2015 to document on-farm practices that, 
while not meeting NRCS standard criteria, are still providing a water quality benefit. Resource 
improvements are found through the following processes: 
 
- 
During the development or when updating a conservation plan. 
- 
During BMP verification of other WIP-eligible BMPs. 
- 
During MACS spot checks or quality assurance reviews. 
- 
During nutrient trading evaluations. 
- 
During agricultural certainty evaluations. 
- 
During Farm Stewardship Certification and Assessment Program Evaluations. 
 
As part of this effort, MDA developed the Non Cost-Shared Best Management Practice and 
Resource Improvement Practice Verification Procedures Manual. In it, soil conservation planners, 
technicians, and other MDA staff are provided certification forms that can be filled out and 
incorporated into a conservation plan to document farmer-installed conservation efforts. This 
requires investigative work, including mandatory visual indicators required for eligibility and 
recordation of WIP-specific data required to pass a certification. In Caroline County, MDA is 
working with SCD staff to track voluntary farm practices. 
 
Developed Land Nitrogen Reductions 
 
Reductions in nitrogen loading from developed land (residential, commercial, institutional and 
industrial) are achieved through implementation of retrofitted urban stormwater management 
BMPs to add to or enhance existing, older stormwater management facilities. Urban BMPs include 
erosion and sediment control practices, constructed wetlands, bioretention areas, rain gardens, 
rooftop drainage connection to a BMP, urban tree planting programs and urban stream 
restoration.  
 
Because stormwater runoff from developed land in Caroline County is unregulated, the 
implementation of BMPs to meet the State’s nitrogen reduction targets for developed land in 
Caroline County is done on a voluntary basis. Implementation of voluntary stormwater BMPs has 
been undertaken by County and municipal governments, non-governmental organizations 
(NGOs), and private property owners.  
 
EPA Clean Water Act Section 319 grants provide funding to states to help communities reduce 
water pollution caused by runoff. The County’s Upper Choptank River Watershed 319(h) 
Watershed Plan developed in 2010, includes strategies for non-point source pollution reduction 
throughout the Upper Choptank River watershed, the County’s largest watershed. In mid-2011, 
the County completed analyses of nutrient loadings for all 70 of its HUC-12 watersheds. These 
analyses were used to identify and prioritize locations where BMP implementation projects offer 
the most opportunity for nutrient reduction and water quality improvement. Most of these 
opportunities were located in watersheds in and around the towns of Denton and Greensboro. In 
late 2011, the County partnered with both towns to develop implementation strategies for

<!-- page 97 -->
84 
 
stormwater BMP projects on developed land.  
 
Since the establishment of the Bay TMDL in 2010, multiple County departments have 
implemented voluntary urban stormwater BMPs on developed land through partnership projects 
with schools, towns, churches, fire departments, and residential property owners; most of these 
are located in the Upper Choptank Watershed. Project scopes have ranged in size from individual 
properties to community-scale projects to large-scale projects that reduced nitrogen impacts from 
hundreds of acres. The County’s largest project to date was implemented in 2022 at the North 
County Regional Park (on the Choptank River immediately north of Greensboro) and included 
construction of a 700-foot regenerative stormwater conveyance system, two Delmarva Bay 
wetlands, multiple bioretention areas and the installation of thousands of native plants and trees. 
The project resulted in a total annual reduction of 409.2 lbs of nitrogen/year, 261.9 lbs of 
phosphorus/year, and 470.9 tons of sediment/year. Additional projects include: 
 
- 
Constructed wetlands, submerged gravel wetlands, rain gardens and native tree and plant 
meadows installed at Greensboro Choptank River Park; 
- 
Bioretention areas and swales installed at the Greensboro Town Office, Public Works 
facility and library; 
- 
Regenerative Stormwater Conveyance system and porous paver parking lot at the 
Greensboro Volunteer Fire Company station and rain garden at the Fire Company 
Community Hall; 
- 
Bioretention facilities at the Caroline County Public Works facility in Denton; 
- 
Bioretention facilities at Lockerman Middle School in Denton; 
- 
Porous asphalt parking lot at Caroline County Department of Emergency Services in 
Denton; 
- 
Community-wide residential BMPs in the Rural Village of Jonestown; 
- 
Rain gardens and grassed swales in Jonestown Community Park; 
- 
Bioretention areas and parking lot porous pavers at Denton Elementary School; 
- 
Stormwater wetlands and bioswales in a neighborhood on Pealiquor Road outside of 
Denton; 
- 
Upland stormwater practices and shoreline restoration at Camp Mardela outside of 
Denton. 
 
The Maryland 319(h) Grant Program and Department of Natural Resources are consistent 
funders of the County’s stormwater and water quality improvement projects. Additional funding 
sources include the Chesapeake Bay Trust and National Fish and Wildlife Foundation. 
 
The County will continue to work with its partners to implement BMPs on developed land to the 
maximum extent possible to insure the State’s nitrogen reduction targets for Caroline County are 
met.

<!-- page 98 -->
85 
 
Septic System Nitrogen Reductions 
 
The State’s recommended BMPs for reduction of nitrogen loads from existing septic systems 
include upgrading conventional systems with Best Available Technology (BAT) units that 
discharge fifty to eighty percent less nitrogen.  Approximately 1,500 septic systems are located in 
the County’s Critical Area. Upgrading all these systems t o B e s t A v a i l a b l e T e c h n o l o g y 
( B A T ) s y s t e m s would result in a t o t a l load reduction of 6,835 pounds of nitrogen per 
year.  
 
The Environmental Health department (a division of MDE within the County Health Department) 
is the permitting and regulating authority for septic systems and manages the State BRF fund for 
septic system BAT system installations in the County. Department sanitarians assist property 
owners with applications to the BRF fund for upgrades and oversee the upgrade process until 
completion. The Department estimates they complete between 20-40 BAT installations per year, 
which includes BAT upgrades to existing systems and new BAT installations. 
 
Approximately $9,000,000 is provided annually statewide for individual septic BAT upgrades and 
ENR treatment plant septic connections, both of which are managed by local health departments.  
Funding is prioritized to cover the cost of the BAT unit and its installation; engineering and design 
costs and necessary drainfield repairs are not eligible for BRF grant funding unless the property 
owner’s annual income is at or below the poverty level. Engineering and design costs average 
$1,000-$3,000 per system; drainfield repairs and upgrades range between $5,000-$20,000 per 
system. This cost burden prevents low, moderate and even middle-income property owners from 
being able to upgrade to BAT technology, even with the BRF grant funding provided for the unit.  
 
The County supports revisions to the BRF policy to provide funding access to property owners for 
the associated costs of a BAT septic upgrade. The County is working with the Envision the 
Choptank partnership to provide information to MDE about this need and the significant increase 
in BAT upgrades that would result.  
 
In lieu of a BAT upgrade, regular pump-outs can achieve a nitrogen reduction of approximately 
1-2 lbs per year from a septic system. The State does not currently require regular septic 
maintenance on most conventional systems (only BAT units and holding tanks), however 
Environmental Health Department staff use public education materials and on-site visits with 
property owners to recommend regular septic pump outs every 2-5 years, depending on usage. 
In cases where prior issues have existed at a site, Department staff may require that a tank be 
uncovered for inspection, and grant approval for an application for Water Supply/Sewer 
Verification (WSV) contingent upon a pump-out. 
 
The State also recommends connecting properties currently served by septic systems to ENR 
wastewater treatment plants, where feasible, as a BMP. The nitrogen reduction achieved by 
connecting a conventional septic system to an ENR treatment plant is approximately double the 
reduction achieved by a BAT septic upgrade. From 2015 through 2019 the County worked with 
the Town of Goldsboro to connect 125 properties served by conventional septic systems to a new 
regional ENR sewer treatment plant owned and operated by the Town of Greensboro. The

<!-- page 99 -->
86 
 
connection of those properties to the new ENR plant resulted in a reduction in the annual septic 
nitrogen load of approximately 1,100 lbs of nitrogen. 
 
Water Quality Implementation 
  
• 
Continue to implement green infrastructure practices on developed land in the County to 
reduce nitrogen impacts from stormwater runoff and protect the habitat value of Caroline 
County’s rivers and streams through: 
 
- 
Large-scale projects on public lands including County parks and buildings and school 
facilities; 
- 
Residential and neighborhood-scale stormwater projects; 
- 
Projects led by the Envision the Choptank partnership that address stormwater flooding 
and water quality impacts in Caroline County. 
 
• 
Continue to utilize County stormwater projects as demonstration projects to provide BMP 
strategies for individual property owners.  
 
• 
Continue to assist municipalities with technical and grant funding resources to implementation 
of BMPs within towns.

<!-- page 100 -->
87 
 
 
 
 
 
The sand and gravel extraction industry in the County provides basic raw materials for the 
construction and paving industries throughout the region, primarily for construction outside of the 
County. The industry plays an important role in supporting local growth and development. 
Because geologic conditions dictate the location of economically recoverable mineral deposits, 
opportunities to meet the future demand of the County’s construction industries will be influenced 
by the availability of these deposits and future access to these deposits for construction industry 
use. 
 
A primary goal of the Caroline County Comprehensive Plan is to manage and conserve the 
County’s mineral resources while protecting surrounding land uses and the environment from 
potential harmful impacts from mining industry activities. Objectives for mineral resources include:  
 
• 
Emphasize to the Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE) the critical importance of 
the reclamation and bonding requirements attached to the mining permits issued by MDE in 
light of the geologic conditions and limitations on re33use of mined land within the County; 
 
• 
Emphasize to MDE the importance of public drainage ditches that cris-cross much of the rural 
land in the County where mining is permitted and the maintenance of which is critical to the 
productive use of the vast majority of such rural land, so that MDE is careful to have any 
mining operation clearly identify such draining systems on the land that is to be mined and will 
include conditions in any mining permit issued that ensures the drainage systems are 
maintained and not adversely impacted by mining operations; 
 
• 
Conserve major accessible mineral resource deposits for future extraction; 
 
• 
Protect current residential areas and future residential growth areas from surface mining 
activities; 
 
• 
Safeguard the public by minimizing impacts resulting from extraction and transport of 
resource deposits; 
 
• 
Protect taxpayers by ensuring that mining and extraction operations provide funding to 
offset the cost of any necessary improvements to infrastructure. 
 
• 
Preserve the agricultural and scenic character of Caroline County; 
 
• 
Preserve productive agricultural land as a renewable resource and the backbone of the local 
economy. 
 
CHAPTER 4: MINERAL RESOURCES

<!-- page 101 -->
88 
 
Caroline County’s sand and gravel is a valuable non-renewable resource that should be extracted 
and used with careful thought regarding the future. These materials are important to both the local 
and national economy. See Md. Code Ann. Envir. § 15-802(a)(1) (stating: “The extraction of 
mineral by mining is a basic and essential activity making an important contribution to 
the economic well-being of the State and nation.”) The General Assembly has recognized that 
while “reasonable steps to protect [such] natural resources from encroachment by other land uses 
that would make [such] resources unavailable for future use,” that desire to protect such resources 
must be balanced “against other possible land uses, including consideration of uses for 
surrounding properties.” Md. Code Ann. Envir. § 15-802(a)(2). Ultimately, the General Assembly 
has recognized how important proper reclamation of mined land is in order “to prevent undesirable 
land and water conditions that would be detrimental to the general welfare, health, safety, beauty 
and property rights of the citizens of the State.” Md. Code Ann. Envir. § 15-802(a)(4). 
 
The geology, hydrology, and topography of Maryland’s Eastern Shore is fundamentally different 
than the balance of the state. Because of the flat topography and highwater table, reclamation 
strategies effective in the Piedmont Plateau may not be in the Coastal Plain geology of Caroline 
County and the Eastern Shore. Pits that have been excavated during mining cannot be filled and 
re-graded for development because they are filled with water and have become ponds after sand 
and gravel mining operations have extracted all usable materials. Given the limitations on 
adaptive reuse of exhausted mines, the location of such operations should be considered 
carefully, particularly when proposed for productive agricultural land. 
 
The Caroline County Code declares the “policy of the County to preserve, protect and encourage 
the development and improvement of its agricultural land for the production of food and other 
agricultural products.” In general, when land that has been productive cropland is mined, such 
land is forever lost to agricultural use, because all that remains at the conclusion of mining is a 
pond. A significant portion of all the land that has been mined in the County was productive 
farmland on which crops had been harvested for decades prior to the conversion of such farms 
to surface mines. The County is concerned about the negative impacts associated with the 
conversion of prime farmland to surface mining. 
 
The County has already achieved protection of mineral resources by maintaining a low-density 
R- Rural zoning district and targeted growth areas through the Transferable Development Rights 
Program. Growth and development should be located appropriately to protect mineral resources, 
and the County’s existing residential areas and targeted growth areas should also be protected 
from incompatible uses such as surface mining. The County should consider prohibiting or 
deterring mining operations near municipalities, the Transferable Development Rights receiving 
areas, and Rural Village Zoning Districts giving careful consideration to the impacts of mining 
operations on residents, public infrastructure, drainage, future uses, and aesthetics. Surface 
mining should continue to be an accepted use in the County permitted by “Special Use Exception” 
and the site plan approval process. Through this process, “quality of life” issues for nearby 
residents should be considered. Reclamation issues and site-specific conditions should be 
addressed during the site plan approval process. Surface mining applications must go through 
site plan review and must conform to the requirements found in 175-27.5 of the Public Local Laws

<!-- page 102 -->
89 
 
of Caroline County.  There are setback and landscaping buffer requirements, hours of operation 
and other requirements that may be imposed by the Planning Commission at time of review and 
approval.  Mining applications must also get Board of Zoning Appeals approval, which may 
include additional requirements. As part of the review process with MDE, the permit must show 
what the end use will be once the mining has been completed on a property. 
 
As of May 2025, Caroline County had 21 active surface mining operations with permits from the 
Maryland Department of the Environment.  
 
Caroline County has strengthened regulations and requirements for mining projects and will 
continue to review projects to ensure that the impact for adjacent properties and the community 
is minimized and that the permit demonstrates the end use for the property.  
 
Planning for Mineral Resources 
 
The Land Use Article of the Annotated Code of Maryland encourages the County to develop a 
Mineral Resources Element to its Comprehensive Plan and to submit that element to MDE for 
review for consistency with the programs and goals of MDE. See Md. Code Ann. Land Use §§ 3- 
102(a)(2) & 3-107. The Mineral Resources Element should “balance mineral resource extraction 
with other land uses; and to the extent feasible … prevent the preemption of mineral resources 
extraction by other uses.” Md. Code. Ann. Land Use. § 3-107(b). The Land Use Article 
encourages the County to ‘identify … appropriate post excavation uses of land that are consistent 
with the County’s land planning process.” Md. Code Ann. Land Use § 3-107(a)(2). 
 
The Maryland Geologic Survey assessed the County for potential mineral resource deposits and 
found extensive deposits of recoverable sand and, to a more limited extent, gravel. Therefore, 
this Comprehensive Plan must include a mineral resources element. In identifying mineral 
containing lands which should be kept undeveloped prior to extraction, there are several factors 
to be considered including the following: 
 
• 
Proximity of significant mineral deposits to sensitive land uses; 
• 
Location of known mineral deposits with respect to areas where growth can go due 
to government investment in infrastructure; 
• 
When the mineral deposits will be extracted; 
• 
Quality of the deposits; 
• 
Ownership of the deposits; 
• 
Expense of mineral operators in exploratory work and documentation of 
recoverable deposits; 
• 
Road access, road and bridge capacity, intersection capacity, projected haul routes, 
and levels of service; 
• 
Public and private infrastructure including utilities and drainage ditches; 
• 
Other viable uses of the land such as agriculture;

<!-- page 103 -->
90 
 
• 
Long term land use plans; 
• 
Topography, drainage, and natural features such as wetlands or tidal-influenced 
waterways; and 
• 
Planned infrastructure such as future road alignments. 
 
Each of these factors should be considered by the County to protect mineral resource deposits 
and to ensure mining operations do not diminish quality of life in Caroline County. 
 
Current Operations

utilize roughly 828.37 acres of land (see Map 1 and Table 1).
