2026 COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

<!-- page 110 -->
97 
 
Current Regulations 
 
MDE regulates surface mining through the State Surface Mining Act, which is Title 15, Subtitle 8 
of the Environment Article, Annotated Code of Maryland and the regulations that MDE has 
promulgated to implement the act. See Md. Code. Ann. Envir §§ 15-801 et seq. & COMAR 
26.21.01.01 et seq. The Surface Mining Act, which was first enacted in 1977 generally requires 
the following: 
 
• 
Any person intending to mine sand and gravel must first obtain a Surface Mining Operator's 
license from the MDE. Licenses must be renewed on an annual basis. 
• 
A surface mining permit must be obtained from MDE before mining commences on a 
particular site. 
• 
Upon completion of the mining operation, the site must be reclaimed in a fashion 
satisfactory to MDE. 
The Surface Mining Act does not pre-empt the County’s zoning authority. To the contrary, the act 
provides that MDE cannot issue a surface mining permit “until the … [C]ounty has completed its 
review of the proposed land use and provided [MDE] with a written statement that … the proposed 
land use conforms with all applicable County zoning and land use requirements.” Md. Code Ann. 
Envir. § 15-810(c). 
Historically, the County has permitted surface mining in the rural, commercial and industrial 
districts of the County provided that a person engaged in mining obtains a special use exception 
pursuant to the applicable provisions of the County Zoning Code and the standards set forth 
therein. The County should comprehensively review its regulatory and development scheme for 
surface mining taking into consideration the noise, dust, truck traffic and aesthetic impacts 
associated with surface mining. 
 
Analysis of Mineral Resources Issues 
 
Current and proposed ordinance provisions should be reviewed and monitored to ensure they 
accomplish three purposes. First, they should distinguish between minor mineral extraction 
(primarily for the purpose of creating an agricultural irrigation pond/reservoir or for on-site use of 
materials by a property owner), and major mineral extraction operations intended primarily for the 
purpose of mining minerals for sale or use in construction or manufacturing. The distinction 
between major and minor mineral extraction operations should follow existing State guidelines 
and Ordinance 2018-1 of the Zoning Code which was updated on April 24, 2018 and included a 
combination of setback, buffer, buffer yard, height and/or minimum acreage requirements for 
minor mineral extraction and major mineral extraction to minimize any potential 
nuisance/detrimental impacts of mineral extraction on neighboring properties.  
 
In Caroline County, because of its geology and relatively flat contours, for over 50 years, public 
drainage ditches that run across private lands have been maintained to increase the amount of 
tillable land and land suitable for development. Maintenance of the integrity of such drainage 
systems is critical to the maintenance of many existing land uses. Additionally, farmers and other

<!-- page 111 -->
98 
 
County landowners have established private drainage ditches that accomplish the same purposes 
as the public drainage ditches created pursuant to the Agricultural Chapter of the Maryland 
Annotated Code. It is critical that these drainage systems are carefully examined during the 
surface mining permitting process. MDE, during its permitting process, should require permit 
applicants to identify all drainage ditches on applicant’s property; to specify how such drainage 
ditches will be maintained, relocated and/or impacted by the proposed mining operations; and to 
undertake whatever actions are necessary to maintain such drainage ditches in the face of the 
proposed mineral extraction activities. An applicant must be required to maintain existing systems 
on their property or to relocate existing systems that interfere with its mining operations and 
ensure that the relocated systems function to handle water discharges that traditionally have been 
handled by such systems. Furthermore, applicants must be precluded from making water and 
sediment discharges to such systems that will overwhelm and degrade such drainage systems 
downstream from applicant’s property. The County’s Department of Planning and Codes will 
support MDE’s surface mining division in accomplishing such objectives. 
 
Surface mining has the potential to detrimentally impact the shallow groundwater aquifer on the 
mined property and neighboring properties. If water is routinely pumped in large quantities from 
excavated areas, whether to allow mining or for washing, processing or other purposes, the 
shallow water aquifer could be drawn down and/or downstream properties could be overwhelmed 
with water discharges. Alternatively, ponded water on mined property could become a resource 
for the irrigation of neighboring farmland or a reservoir of public water supply. MDE is encouraged 
to work cooperatively with the Department of Agriculture, the Department of Natural Resources 
and the County in identifying productive uses of ponded water on mined land.  Planning 
Commission - should there be any additional recommendations? 
 
Special Use Exception Conditions 
 
The current Special Use Exception process required operators to establish mining activities on a 
site are minimal and the Zoning Ordinance provides no specific guidance in the review and 
approval of such activities. The County updated the Zoning Ordinance on April 24, 2018, under 
Ordinance 2018-1 and addressed the following: 
 
• 
Minimum setbacks from roads and parcel lines for excavating. 
• 
Minimum setbacks from roads and parcel lines for the storage of materials, structures, 
and other activities related to extraction operations. 
• 
Requirements for natural vegetated buffers between surface mining operations and roads, 
adjoining parcels, and edges of streams. 
• 
A traffic study may be required as part of the site plan approval process. Improvements 
to the roads, drainage, and/or intersections the extraction operation will use or impact may 
be required. 
• 
A performance bond for any damage done by an extraction operation to public 
infrastructure may be required. 
• 
Restrictions may be imposed on hours and/or days of operation.

<!-- page 112 -->
99 
 
• 
Dust or noise abatement. 
• 
Haul route requirements may be imposed to ensure truck or equipment traffic does not 
damage County roads. 
• 
Any extraction operation which falls within the Critical Area shall be referred to the Critical 
Area Commission for review and approval. Any requirements established by the Critical 
Area Commission shall be included in the approved site plan.   
 
Protection of the Natural Environment 
 
The County's Critical Area Protection program, State Surface Mining Law, and measures 
recommended in this Plan for sensitive areas protection provide a framework for review of 
proposed mineral extraction activities. These existing laws and regulations establish protection 
measures for sensitive environmental features and are monitored as part of the permitting 
process. 
 
Mining operators currently must comply with State environmental regulations relating to operating 
and closure requirements. County concerns related to environmental issues should be raised 
during the State’s environmental assessment. Any duplication between State and local 
ordinances should be limited to information related to the environmental impact assessment, 
particularly for control of sediment and erosion. While both processes require similar information, 
the distinction between the regulatory purposes served by both State and County reviews appear 
to be justified. The State reviews information primarily to determine appropriate engineering and 
technical design. The County is responsible for on- and off-site land use, health, safety, and 
general welfare impacts; including traffic, dust, noise, odor, use intensity, disturbance to adjacent 
land uses and the aesthetics of mining operations. The County has the authority to enforce 
conditions of site plan approval and local zoning, and development laws intended to protect the 
public health, safety and welfare of County residents and neighboring property owners. 
 
The State of Maryland requires special protection for waters of very high quality (Tier II waters). 
The policies and procedures that govern these special waters are commonly called “anti-
degradation policies.” This policy states that “proposed amendments to County plans or 
discharge permits for discharge to Tier II waters that will result in a new, or an increased, permitted 
annual discharge of pollutants and a potential impact to water quality, shall evaluate alternatives 
to eliminate or reduce discharges or impacts.” Information on Tier II waters can be obtained online 
at: http://www.dsd.state.md.us/comar/comarhtml/26/26.08.02.04.htm and policy implementation 
procedures are located at http://www.dsd.state.md.us/comar/comarhtml/26/26.089.02.04-1.ht5m. 
 
Reclamation and Post Excavation Use 
 
Reclamation and post excavation use of mined property is of significant concern to the County. 
The County would like to see mined land turned into recreation land for hunting, fishing, camping 
and/or similar outdoor activities that are consistent with County land use policies and regulations. 
This would require coordination at the initial permitting stage for the purpose of ensuring that

<!-- page 113 -->
100 
 
habitats conducive to fishing or attracting ducks, geese, and/or deer for future hunting are 
developed during the interim reclamation process and so that final reclamation prepares the 
mined property for such future uses where applicable. 
 
To ensure mining sites are restored to a usable state, appropriate action must be taken before, 
during, and after extraction. The MDE permitting process for surface mining requires reclamation 
plans for any mined site. In addition, bonds are posted to ensure the availability of funds for 
reclamation should an operator abandon the site. Since the licensing process was established, 
these restorative guidelines and regulations have been successful in ensuring the restoration of 
sites where extraction has taken place. 
 
As part of the application process, the post-extraction intended use of the property must be 
identified. The restoration plan should be consistent with the future land use of the site. The 
following are recommended post extraction land uses: 
 
• 
Recreational land use: parks and lakes; 
• 
Forestry; 
• 
Farmland; 
• 
Aquaculture; 
• 
Low density residential development; and 
• 
Disposal of non-toxic solid fill material; clean fill material and disposal of inorganic solid 
fill material. 
Inventory Abandoned Extraction Sites 
Reclamation of abandoned extraction sites (i.e. pre-Natural Resources Law) also is a concern for 
unrestored mining sites for which no closure bond was required. An initial step toward reclaiming 
these abandoned sites would be to conduct an inventory and evaluation of all abandoned sites to 
determine reclamation needs and their potential for other land uses. Working to get information 
from Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE) on inventory and status.  The reclamation 
process is handled through MDE, but most permits are extended.   If in the future existing sites 
are abandoned, the County is requesting that MDE notify the County so that we may be aware of 
the status of these sites. 
Reducing Impacts on County Infrastructure 
Extraction operations may have a significant impact on the County road system. Mining 
operations, including heavy truck traffic, can damage low-capacity roads and create safety 
hazards and disturbance to neighbors and travelers on these routes. The County may require 
developers of mining operations to improve roads to adequately handle heavier vehicles and 
traffic volumes. 
Where mineral resource operations are proposed for rural areas, the operators of such facilities 
should be expected to underwrite upgrades or repairs along established haul routes for trucks

<!-- page 114 -->
101 
 
servicing the operations. In doing so, improvements to the transportation system could be made 
before truck traffic from mining operations begins to overload substandard or undersized 
roadways. 
Mineral Resources Implementation 
 
• 
Assure available measures are taken to protect the natural environment from all 
sources of pollution resulting from mineral extraction activities; 
 
• 
Assure available measures are taken during the application review process, including 
notification of adjoining jurisdictions of proposed expansion or establishment mining 
operations, to protect County and local roads from increased stress due to heavier loads 
and additional traffic that could impact the integrity of existing road networks; 
 
• 
Require post-excavation uses for mined sites be consistent with County plans and regulations; 
 
• 
Utilize appropriate methods for protecting existing neighborhoods from the impacts of 
adjacent extraction operations and the transportation of extracted resources; 
 
• 
Provide adequate regulation and monitoring of mineral extraction operations to assure 
compliance with applicable permitting requirements; 
 
• 
Identify and utilize any programs which can support reclamation or reforestation of older 
borrow pits or mined sites which were not subject to reclamation requirements established by 
the Surface Mining Law; 
 
• 
Review zoning districts relative to surface mining to protect mineral resources, agriculture, 
and current and future residential areas; 
 
• 
Monitor the effectiveness of current zoning provisions and special use exception conditions 
and additional requirements put in place with the Zoning Code update under Ordinance 2018-
1;  
 
• 
Create and update, as necessary, an inventory of abandoned extraction sites; working with 
MDE on obtaining information; 
 
• 
Encourage expansion of sites in areas of existing operations and/or near inactive/abandoned 
sites as an alternative to establishing new ones;    
 
• 
Adopt and maintain land use policies and regulations that discourage the preemption of 
mineral extraction by other uses;

<!-- page 115 -->
102 
 
 
• 
With the update of the Zoning Code, review and consider revising existing zoning regulations 
to address residential safety and disturbance concerns and to add additional requirements to 
ensure compatibility with uses in surrounding neighborhoods. For site plans and Board of 
Zoning Appeals applications, additional information shall be included to determine if different 
requirements should be implemented based on the size and type of mining process, location, 
size of property, the scope of the operation, proximity to adjacent roads and properties along 
with the use of the adjacent property, and also to require submittal of an operations plan. 
Other items that may need to be taken into consideration for approvals can be considered at 
that time. Currently the requirements for any mining operation are the same but the process 
is different based on a minor application (five acres or less) or a major application (above five 
acres);  
 
• 
Consider additional fees to allow a part-time contracted inspector to conduct annual 
inspections to ensure that all County requirements (in the Code or additional conditions 
required by the Planning Commission and/or the Board of Zoning Appeals) are being adhered 
to.  Add requirement on applications for signed permission to access property with a mining 
operation at any time; 
 
• 
Consider requirement in writing that if the property fronts on a State Road and a County road 
that the applicant/owner has pursed all avenues through Maryland State Highway for access 
on the State road before granting access on a County road; and  
 
• 
With the update of the Zoning Code, define “loads” and “trips” when a load or trip limitation is 
placed as part of the approval process and include that as required information on site plan 
and Board of Zoning Appeals applications so that it is clear for all reviewing agencies and 
boards as well as the owner and applicant. Review and adopt reasonable enforcement 
measures of the maximum number permitted and hours of operation.

<!-- page 116 -->
103 
 
 
 
Caroline County established its Priority Preservation Area in 2010 as a requirement of Maryland’s 
Agricultural Stewardship Act, enacted in 2006. The Act required counties seeking to certify their State 
farmland preservation programs to amend their comprehensive plans with a designated Priority 
Preservation Area (PPA) intended to protect and support agricultural and forestry enterprises and 
deflect development to more suitable areas. 
 
In 2025 the State abandoned its long-held agricultural land preservation priorities in favor of 
renewable energy development, to the extent that Caroline County’s agriculture-based economy and 
the land it relies on are now threatened. The 2025 Renewable Energy Certainty Act, which 
requires counties to permit community and utility-scale solar development in designated Priority 
Preservation Areas, preempts local government control over the location and construction of solar 
and other renewable energy-generating facilities. The act establishes a requirement for counties to 
permit ground-mounted community and utility-scale solar arrays on up to 5% of the total acreage within 
a county's designated PPA.  In Carolne County this would impact approximately 6400 acres of 
predominantly prime farmland, which would be vulnerable to the development of large-scale solar 
arrays and electrical sub-stations that will occupy the landscape for 20 to 30 years at least.  
 
"Under the Agricultural Stewardship Act, counties certified by the Maryland Department of Planning 
(MDP) and the Maryland Agricultural Land Preservation Foundation (MALPF) retain 75% of the 
locally generated agricultural land transfer tax. Certified counties commit to protecting at least 80% 
of the undeveloped acreage within a county's PPA." Based on current GIS data, the County’s total 
PPA is 127,471 acres; approximately 25,300 acres of the PPA is comprised of smaller lot 
developed areas; 80% of the remaining 102,171 acres of undeveloped land totals approximately 
82,000 acres. Caroline County’s primary goal for the Priority Preservation Area is to preserve these 
undeveloped acres and protect them from encroachment of non-agricultural uses to the maximum 
extent possible. 
 
Objectives to achieve this goal include: 
 
• 
Enact County legislation to provide an additional mechanism for funding permanent 
preservation easements on agricultural land. 
 
• 
Work with municipalities, counties, allied agencies and non-government and non-profit 
organizations to coordinate legislative efforts to reduce local impacts from the Renewable 
Energy Certainty Act and other State mandates that threaten Caroline County’s farmland and 
agriculture-based economy. 
 
• 
Review and revise the County codes to ensure that regulations support economic 
opportunities for farms and agricultural businesses to the maximum extent possible. 
 
• 
Prioritize protection of Prime Farmland and Farmland of Statewide Importance from non-
CHAPTER 5: Priority Preservation Area

<!-- page 117 -->
104 
 
agricultural uses. 
 
• 
Enhance coordination for agricultural initiatives among Municipal, County, State, and Federal 
entities and private landowners. 
 
• 
Continue to support public and private preservation programs and conservation initiatives. 
 
Current Priority Preservation Area Program 
 
The County’s original Priority Preservation Area of 176,760 acres was established in the 2010 
Comprehensive Plan. The PPA included all land in the unincorporated area not designated for 
future development, which included the TDR receiving area, municipal growth areas and Priority 
Funding Areas. 
 
The original PPA was reduced in size in 2012 as a result of the County’s Comprehensive Rezoning 
process, which revised zoning districts to more effectively manage growth in developed areas. 
The PPA boundary was revised in response to the changes, which included: 
 
• 
The removal of areas surrounding Rural Village Priority Funding Areas that were re-zoned 
to the new Village Center (VC) and Village Neighborhood (VN) zoning districts. 
• 
The removal of all areas not zoned R-Rural (the original PPA included areas in residential, 
commercial and industrial zoning districts). 
• 
The removal of mobile home parks that were rezoned to the MH-Mobile Home Park district. 
Additional acreage removed from the PPA included: 
 
• 
Areas annexed into the Town of Marydel. 
• 
Parcels protected by permanent conservation or agricultural preservation easements. 
• 
Parcels rezoned to correct split-zoning designations. 
 
The revisions resulted in a reduction of the PPA from 176,760 acres County-wide to 126,468 
acres (127,471.05 acres based on current GIS data) in the R-Rural zoning district (see Map 4-1, 
Priority Preservation Area). Of the approximately 50,000 acres of land removed from the PPA, 
approximately 40,000 acres were parcels permanently preserved by protective easements. 
 
The Planning Commission will review the PPA every five years as part of the County’s Maryland 
Agricultural Land Preservation Foundation (MALPF) recertification process and remove acres that 
have been placed under permanent protective easement, including but not limited to MALPF, 
Rural Legacy Area and Maryland Environmental Trust easements.

<!-- page 119 -->

Priority Preservation Area Program Effectiveness 
 
Even before the establishment of the PPA, the County prioritized the preservation of agricultural 
land as the foundation of its agriculture-based economy as well as its historic identity and 
landscape. The County’s agricultural landscape is its most valuable resource and County land 
use policy and regulations emphatically support its preservation. The County has a significant 
amount of Class I and Class II soils, which yield high crop production. Class 1 and Class II soils  
 
are ideal for a wide range of field crops and require minimal management to achieve high 
productivity. They are deep, well-drained, and hold moisture well, making them the best 
agricultural soils available. 
 
Class I and Class II soils comprise the majority of the County’s Prime Farmland and Farmland of 
Statewide Importance, which are agricultural areas designated by the US Department of 
Agriculture (USDA). 
 
USDA classifies 88,615.52 acres, or about 45%, of Caroline County as Prime Farmland. Prime 
Farmland is of major importance in meeting the nation's short- and long-range needs for food and 
fiber. Because the supply of high-quality farmland is limited, the U.S. Department of Agriculture 
recognizes that “responsible levels of government, as well as individuals, should encourage and 
facilitate the wise use of our Nation's prime farmland.” 
 
USDA classifies an additional 71,649.09 acres, or about 36%, of Caroline County as Farmland of 
Statewide Importance, which is considered to be farmland of statewide importance for the 
production of food, feed, fiber, forage, and oilseed crops. Some areas of Statewide Importance 
may produce as high a yield as Prime Farmland if conditions are favorable. 
 
Approximately 81% (103,771 acres) of the Priority Preservation Area is comprised of Prime 
Farmland and Farmland of Statewide Importance (primarily Class I and Class II soils). 
 
USDA notes that the loss of prime farmland to other uses puts pressure on marginal agricultural 
lands, which generally are more erodible, droughty, and less productive and cannot be easily 
cultivated – potentially risking the stability of short- and long-range supplies of food and fiber. 
 
According to the requirements of the Renewable Energy Certainty Act, approximately 6,300 
acres, or 5%, of the County’s PPA must be accessible for commercial solar development with 
minimal restrictions on siting and development review. Map 4-2 (Priority Preservation Area and 
Solar Facilities) illustrates the locations of commercial solar facility projects currently in various 
stages of development throughout the County, most of which are located within the PPA. Most of 
this farmland is leased by the solar developer for periods of 20 to 30 years; some of the farmland 
is purchased outright. Construction impacts, including removal of topsoil, soil compaction, and 
grading that will alter natural topography and drainage, have the potential to reduce productivity 
on the land if it is ever returned to agricultural use. 
 
The greatest pressure on preservation of the County’s agricultural land is from community and

<!-- page 121 -->
108 
 
utility-scale commercial solar development. Permanent preservation easements are the most 
effective means of protecting farmland from encroachment of commercial solar and other non-
agricultural uses. The Renewable Energy Certainty Act prohibits solar development on land 
protected by permanent conservation or agricultural preservation easement. 
 
The County administers the Maryland Agricultural Land Preservation Foundation (MALPF) 
easement program in Caroline County and manages over 250 permanent MALPF easements 
that, as of 2025, protect over 36,000 acres (see Map 4-3, Land Protected by Permanent 
Easement). Caroline County has participated in the MALPF Program since 1980 and ranks 
second in the State for number of acres preserved under the program. 
 
The State’s FY26 budget reduced funding for the MALPF program to a level that will significantly 
reduce the number of permanent agricultural easements the County will be able to secure in the 
upcoming funding cycle. The State projects similar reduced funding levels for the program over 
the next five fiscal cycles, which will impact the County’s ability to permanently preserve 
agricultural land through 2030. 
 
The Eastern Shore Land Conservancy administers the State’s Rural Legacy Area (RLA) 
easement program, which funds permanent easements on rural and agricultural land. RLA 
easements are eligible in State-designated Rural Legacy Areas. There are two RLAs in Caroline 
County, both located largely within the County’s PPA: the Tuckahoe RLA and the Eastern Shore 
Heartland RLA (see Map 4-3, Land Protected by Permanent Easement). In 2025 there are a total 
of 23 RLA easements protecting 3,917 acres with the two Rural Legacy Areas in Caroline County. 
 
The Rural Legacy Area program is administered by the Maryland Department of Natural 
Resources and, like the MALPF easement program, was impacted by State funding cuts in the 
FY26 budget. The RLA program also is forecast to receive significantly reduced State funding 
over the next several fiscal cycles, which will reduce the number of permanent easements ESLC 
will be able to secure on Caroline County farms. 
 
In 2025 the County created the Agricultural Land Preservation Fund, dedicated to the preservation 
of agricultural land containing Class I or II soils (soil with high productivity for producing food crops 
and, thus, of national importance for preservation as farmland). The payment of a monetary 
Compensatory Preservation Contribution from all applicants for solar energy generating 
stations/systems and energy storage devices is required for project areas that include land 
classified as either Class I or Class II soils. All Compensatory Preservation Contributions for 
agricultural land preservation collected by the County will be deposited in the Agricultural Land 
Preservation Fund for Caroline County. Money in the Fund may only be used for the purchase of 
permanent agricultural land preservation easements, including but not limited to MALPF 
easements. 
 
In addition to permanent easement programs, the County’s Transfer of Development Rights 
(TDR) program protects the County’s rural and agricultural areas from large-scale residential and 
commercial solar development. When development rights are lifted through the County's TDR 
program, a perpetual easement is placed on the original property which permanently prevents

<!-- page 123 -->
110 
 
future residential and commercial solar buildout. Approximately 3,000 acres of rural agricultural 
land are protected from development through the County’s TDR program. 
 
The County Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) program, discussed in the Land Use chapter, 
will continue to play a role in the County’s land preservation efforts. The creation of the TDR 
Program eliminated major residential subdivisions in the R-Rural zoning district except in the 
designated TDR receiving area, which now totals approximately 4,600 acres. This was a 
significant step towards directing growth away from farmland and undeveloped rural areas. 
Although the program has the potential to be an effective tool for deterring sprawl development, 
the County has not experienced significant residential development pressure since the early 
2000s. The lack of residential development in the County has resulted in a potential shortage of 
available housing to meet short and long-term future population needs. The projected housing 
shortage, which is more significant at State and national levels, will require the County to assess 
additional opportunities for residential development in areas that are designated as suitable for 
growth. The assessment will be conducted as part of the County’s comprehensive rezoning 
process (to take place following the update of the Comprehensive Plan) and will include a review 
of the TDR receiving area and coordination with municipalities on joint housing and growth 
planning initiatives. 
 
During the 2012 Comprehensive Rezoning process, the County downzoned most of the 
undeveloped land in the higher density R-1 Residential zoning district to R-Rural, to further direct 
non-agricultural uses away from agricultural areas and to areas designated for development. 
 
In 2014, the County adopted its Septic Growth Tiers in compliance with Maryland’s Sustainable 
Growth and Agricultural Preservation Act. The State developed four tiers of growth categories to 
identify where major and minor residential subdivisions may be located in counties and towns and 
what type of sewerage system (public sewerage or on-site septic) will serve them. Septic Tiers 
range in allowed density from Tier I, the highest density (development on sewer) to Tier IV, areas 
planned for preservation and conservation. To preserve agricultural land as the County’s 
dominant land use and support the local, agriculture-based economy, the County designated over 
90% of its land area as Tier IV. 
 
In 2015, the County successfully applied for certification as a MALPF Certified County to increase 
the percentage of agricultural land transfer tax the County can retain for use as local matching 
funds in the MALPF easement program. The MALPF certification resulted in an increase from 
25% to 75% of agricultural transfer tax revenues dedicated to the purchase of permanent 
easements on farmland.

Priority Preservation Area Implementation 
 
The County will continue to prioritize the preservation of vital farmland in the Priority Preservation 
Area even as the State continues to facilitate its loss to commercial solar development. The 
County’s strategies to implement its preservation goals will necessarily rely on support from local 
and statewide partners to ensure that protection of farmland is a successful, statewide effort. 
 
Strategies to support this effort include: 
 
• 
Provide testimony and data to assist with the development of local and State legislation that 
will reduce local impacts from the Renewable Energy Certainty Act and other State mandates 
that threaten farmland and agriculture-based economies. 
 
• 
Work with the County Agricultural Land Preservation Advisory Board to update the County’s 
MALPF ranking criteria to include additional points for properties located within the Priority 
Preservation Area and/or within two miles of an electric transmission line. 
 
• 
Assist ESLC with increasing farmer participation in the Rural Legacy Area easement program 
on farms within the Priority Preservation Area. 
 
• 
Coordinate contributions from the County Agricultural Land Preservation Fund to the MALPF 
and/or Rural Legacy Area programs for purchase of permanent easements in Caroline 
County. 
 
• 
Establish increased opportunities for agriculture-based businesses via zoning permissions as 
part of the County’s Comprehensive Rezoning process.

<!-- page 125 -->
112 
 
• 
Establish a joint growth management planning program with municipalities to identify 
opportunities to direct development away from agricultural areas and towards planned 
municipal growth areas.

<!-- page 126 -->
113 
 
 
 
The Transportation Element of the Caroline County Comprehensive Plan addresses the existing 
and proposed needs of Caroline County citizens regarding transportation infrastructure. The goals 
of the Transportation Element of the Caroline County Comprehensive Plan are to provide for the 
safe and efficient movement of people and goods and encourage regional and local coordination 
of transportation and communications decisions. Objectives include: 
 
• 
Maintain and improve a functional road and street system for the safe, convenient, and 
efficient movement of people and goods along the County and State Road networks; 
 
• 
Require road improvements for major developments that will impact County Roads; 
 
• 
Conduct regular maintenance County roads, and bridges and their stormwater and flood 
management networks based on a priority roads list to be updated annually; 
 
• 
Coordinate local transportation planning with County, State and Federal efforts to provide an 
efficient transportation system, improve safety for motorists, and control access points along 
State and County roads; 
 
• 
Support initiatives and opportunities for improved access to public transportation; and 
 
• 
Support the development of alternative modes of travel such as pedestrian and bicycle routes 
within Priority Funding Areas and designated growth areas. 
 
State Highways 
 
 
Major highway access routes in Caroline County include MD Routes 16, 404, 480, 311, 312, 313, 
328 and 331. There are 19 State Highways that total approximately 165 miles. The existing State 
highway system provides for easy connections from Caroline County to higher order roads such 
as US Routes 50, 301, and 13 that access metropolitan areas in Maryland and Delaware (see 
Map 6-1 Roads). 
 
Access to major metropolitan areas was enhanced when segments of MD Route 404 were 
dualized (see next section). The County and Maryland State Department of Transportation have 
listed the final phase of MD 404 dualization as a priority in the State Highway Prioritization Plan. 
The closest regional cities include Dover and Wilmington, Delaware; Philadelphia, Pennsylvania; 
Annapolis and Baltimore, Maryland; and Washington D.C., all located within 2 to 3 hours driving 
time from areas within the County. 
 
MD Route 404, a major arterial route, bisects the County from east to west connecting with federal 
highways in Maryland (US 50) and Delaware (US 13). Another state highway, MD Route 16, 
bisects the southern half of the County from Denton through Preston, connecting to Dorchester 
County. MD Routes 313, 311 and 454 in the northern half of the County, and Route 318 in the

Maryland Highway Needs Inventory 
 
The Highway Needs Inventory (HNI) is a long-term, technical reference and planning document 
that identifies State highway improvements to serve existing and projected population and 
economic activity in the state. The State Regional and Intermodal Planning Division (RIPD) works 
with counties and other MDOT SHA offices and districts to update the HNI triennially. 
 
The County is committed to working in partnership with the Maryland Department of 
Transportation (MDOT) to advance key transportation projects that improve safety, support 
economic growth, and enhance the quality of life for our residents. Based on the Highway Needs 
Inventory for Caroline County and as part of the FY2025 Consolidated Transportation Program, 
the County has identified and ranked the following projects in order of priority. These rankings 
reflect both ongoing needs and urgent infrastructure concerns and the County requested that 
MDOT and SHA give priority consideration to the following: 
 
Priority A: Maintain and Improve Existing Infrastructure: 
The County has identified State roads that require immediate attention due to deteriorating 
surface conditions and insufficient drainage: 
• 
Comprehensive resurfacing of MD 313 from MD 404 to Federalsburg, including repairs to 
pavement, shoulders, and stormwater infrastructure. 
• 
Completion of the resurfacing project on MD Route 16 from Lake Drive to MD 404 
• 
General resurfacing, shoulder repair, drainage improvement, and signage upgrades 
across Caroline County’s state road network. 
 
Priority B: Widen MD 404 from Harmony Road to MD 16 East: 
Caroline County continues to prioritize the final phase of the MD 404 dualization. Previous phases 
have led to improved traffic flow and reduced accidents, demonstrating the importance of 
completing the corridor. Additional needs include: 
• 
Begin preliminary planning and design 
• 
Allocate funding for engineering and right-of-way acquisition 
• 
Engage with local stakeholders for project coordination 
This segment is essential for ensuring safe and efficient east-west travel across the mid-
shore region. 
 
Priority C: Rebuild and Resurface MD 454 (Crownstone Road) – Marydel to Templeville: 
MD 454 is a vital connector for residents in the western part of the County and serves as a 
gateway to Queen Anne’s County. This route has seen substantial wear and requires resurfacing 
and structural upgrades to ensure safe and dependable travel. 
 
Priority D: Rebuild and Resurface MD 313 – Greensboro to Goldsboro: 
This portion of MD 313 is a major north-south route within the County and is essential for local

<!-- page 129 -->
116 
 
mobility and emergency response. Resurfacing and drainage improvements are necessary to 
restore safe travel conditions and extend the life of the corridor. 
 
Priority E: Restripe MD 317 and Right Turn Lane 
Restriping MD 317 (Burrsville Road) at the intersection of MD 313 to add a right turn lane will help 
enhance traffic flow and improve safety for all drivers. 
 
The Maryland Department of Transportation/State Highway Administration (MDOT/SHA) has a 
list of current priority paving projects. The recently submitted priority list will be reviewed alongside 
the existing priorities to coordinate efforts and address the most urgent infrastructure needs. The 
current state paving projects are listed below: 
 
•

County Roads 
 
There are 481 miles of County-owned roads, approximately 77 of which are unpaved. The 
Department of Public Works has three categories for roads: Primary, Secondary and Tertiary. 
Primary roads are hard-surface roads with the highest traffic counts that either connect two state 
roads and/or serve as a bypass around a municipality. Secondary roads are hard-surface roads 
with lower traffic counts that primarily serve the local neighborhoods. Tertiary roads can be hard- 
surface or gravel roads with very low traffic counts that may also serve as a single-access road 
into a community. 
 
Various evolving factors are significantly accelerating the deterioration of this infrastructure. 
These include shifts in traffic patterns, road conditions, vehicle types, and weather events, as well 
as an increase in overall traffic volume. Notable contributing factors include: 
• 
The use of larger and heavier agricultural equipment and commercial transport vehicles 
• 
Increased volume of residential trash collection trucks and frequency of service. 
• 
The emergence and continued growth of home delivery services such as Amazon Prime, 
Door Dash, Instacart and meal delivery providers.

<!-- page 130 -->
117 
 
• 
An increase in commercial deliveries, driven in part by the adoption of new regulations 
permitting home trucking operations and cottage industries. 
• 
Changes in school bus routes, with many districts implementing door-to-door student 
pickup rather than centralized stops. 
• 
The increasing frequency and severity of storms, which contribute to drainage issues as 
well as pavement, roadbed, and culvert degradation. 
• 
A persistent lack of funding due to the discontinuation of the State Highway User Fee, 
which previously supported municipal and county road maintenance. 
• 
The widespread use of GPS navigation apps, which divert both passenger and 
commercial vehicles onto local road networks in an effort to bypass congestion on state 
highways. 
• 
Prolonged exposure to sunlight and longer periods of high temperatures degrades asphalt 
binders, making pavement more brittle and susceptible to cracking – particularly in areas 
with minimal tree cover or where shade-providing trees have been removed. 
 
Priorities for County roads include mitigation of impacts from increasingly frequent stormwater 
and tidal flooding by improving unpaved roadway surfaces and their drainage systems, elevating 
vulnerable roadways in tidal areas, and improving road culverts to reduce incidences of flooding 
and blowouts. 
 
• 
The Caroline County Department of Public Works is alerting motorists of upcoming tar- 
and-chip operations on its “Caroline County Government” Facebook page. 
• 
The department maintains over 450 miles of roadway and 39 bridges or crossings, 
including routine maintenance, line striping, mowing shoulders, repairing and replacing 
bridges, installing and maintaining signs and other safety improvements. 
• 
The Caroline County Department of Public Works is currently researching advanced 
software and technology solutions to enhance traffic data collection across the County. 
The data collected will be utilized to develop a prioritized list for improvements to primary 
roads, ensuring that resources are allocated efficiently. 
 
Flooding in the County, particularly riverine flooding, has recently been analyzed as a high priority 
hazard risk in the 2024 Caroline County Hazard Mitigation Plan (CCHMP). “The flat topography 
of the County, combined with its humid climate, high seasonal water tables, and generally poorly 
drained soils, produce natural flood problems, such as the control and disposal of surface water 
caused by abnormally high rainfall and conveyance issues” (CCHMP). The County has seen 
flooding problems and associated transportation, and ecological problems have increased for 
several road types. The County will analyze and design resilience improvements for three types 
of predominant roadway flooding issues in the County to both improve road resilience as quickly 
as possible for several critical roadways as well as develop methods for incrementally addressing 
road resilience in more locations throughout the County. 
 
While the County is facing persistent flood hazards, additional funding is needed to bring the 
surface transportation resilience in the County to a level where roads are not being routinely 
damaged by flooding. The County has developed a road priorities narrative and database for use

<!-- page 131 -->
118 
 
in applying for state, federal and regional transportation funding to assist with design and 
construction of identified priority projects. It is anticipated that these efforts will continue over the 
next several fiscal cycles until priority road segment projects have been completed. 
 
Road Raising projects: in 2024, the County identified priority road segments located in proximity 
to rivers and streams which are frequently flooded and washed-out during storm and high tide 
events. These wash-outs pose a threat to public safety and require road closures, which impact 
on the mobility of citizens, emergency vehicles and school buses while the road is being repaired. 
The County proposes to raise these road segments to ensure resiliency and more rapid recovery 
after tidal and storm flooding events. In addition to increasing long-term resilience of the roads, 
when completed the road raising project will reduce annual and repeat flooding event 
maintenance needs. 
 
Protection of the road network is also addressed in the Mineral Resources chapter of the 
Comprehensive Plan. Safeguarding the existing roads from the high impact traffic associated with 
mineral mining operations and solar development is a priority. As part of the site plan and 
permitting process, route requirements and road conditions are reviewed. Necessary 
improvements, repairs or bonding measures are incorporated into project approvals. Following 
the adoption of the Comprehensive Plan, additional protective conditions may be considered 
during the Zoning Code update. 
 
Unpaved Roads 
 
The County owns over 70 miles of unpaved (dirt and gravel) roads, located throughout the County 
and often in close proximity to streams (see Map 6-2 Unpaved Roads). Maintenance of these 
roads requires extensive manhours and resources; stormwater runoff from unpaved roads 
contributes significant sediment loads to the rivers and streams annually. Unpaved roads can 
prohibit the rapid delivery of emergency services including police, fire and ambulance response 
to rural areas. In 2024 multiple County departments worked with the Board of Education to index 
and prioritize the County’s 63 unpaved roads based on multiple factors, including the number of 
residences located on the road, types of vehicular traffic accessing the road, proximity to 
waterways, frequency of emergency services calls on the road, and distance to the Department 
of Public Works roads facility in Denton. Proposed improvements to these roads include surface 
and drainage upgrades to improve road network resiliency during storm events, ensure the safety 
and reliability of the County’s transportation network and protect water quality in nearby streams 
and rivers. 
 
Surface conditions of unpaved roads create logistical challenges for County services, particularly 
Emergency Management, the Fire Department, and Police. In 2024, staff from Planning and 
Codes, and the Department of Public Works worked with several other agencies to develop 
specific criteria to use as a matrix to prioritize upgrades to unpaved County Roads. The following 
items were used for the ranking of that list: 
 
• 
NWI Points – the National Wetlands Inventory (NWI), which is a dataset that provides 
information on wetlands, including their characteristics, functions, and locations.

<!-- page 132 -->
119 
 
• 
Internal Flood Points – roads that can reach flood stages due to events within their 
watersheds, including storm surges that can lead to tidal flooding. 
• 
Department of Public Works identified Flood/Wash Areas – based on past data and 
patterns and D P W staff verification of flood and washout areas. 
• 
Department of Public Works Identified Issue Areas – DP W staff listed specific issues on 
some roads, such as narrow right-of-way or guardrail concerns. 
• 
Board of Education Priority Bus Routes/Access to Schools – the BOE listed priority roads 
that have problems that impact school bus trips. 
• 
Department of Emergency Services points – DES ranked the roads based on call and 
response data. 
• 
DES EMS Priority (Calls on road 2023) – DES and Emergency Medical Services provided 
the number of emergency calls for each road. 
 
The roads were “scored” using the collected data and prioritized for upgrading from unpaved to 
paved surfaces and improved drainage systems. The prioritized list of needs for unpaved County 
roads will be used for state and federal grant applications for transportation funding; the list is 
flexible and may be adjusted depending on available funding or changes in road conditions. 
 
Bridge and Culvert and Repairs 
 
With the cooperation of the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS), the County identified several 
culvert bridge crossings which currently experience repeat flooding blowouts, and which have 
been identified by USFWS as barrier culverts in anadromous fish spawning areas. Barrier culverts 
have restricted widths which can clog with debris, causing flooding or blowouts, which create 
public safety hazards, disrupt the transportation network and impede the migration of fish. The 
County plans to acquire funds to redesign the identified culvert bridges to reduce flooding and 
blowout events and restore natural stream flow and fish passage under the roadway. These 
culvert retrofit projects restore healthy habitat for migratory fish and ensure ecological continuity 
in the streams, floodplains, and wetlands which cover Caroline County. 
 
Crash Data 
 
According to crash data for Caroline County provided by the Maryland Highway Safety Office, 
between 2020 and 2024 crashes and injuries increased each year with a rise in 2023 and 2024 
of fatalities resulting from crashes. More than half of all crashes and injuries and fatalities resulting 
from crashes occur on State Highways. Although records indicate that traffic congestion increases 
during the summer months on MD Route 404, the month of the year does not appear to have 
significant influence on crashes or injuries or fatalities resulting from crashes. The most crashes 
and crashes resulting in injuries occur around 7 a.m. and between noon and 6 p.m., while fatalities 
from crashes appear more sporadic and Saturdays generating the most accidents for a 24 hour 
period. 
 
Drivers between the ages of 16 and 20 make up the highest percentage of driver fatalities, 26%. 
Based on the 2024 population, 3.4% of Caroline County drivers are in this age bracket. and have 
the highest percentage of driver injuries at 36. Since 2019, distracted driving has been associated

<!-- page 133 -->
120 
 
with crashes resulting in serious injuries and fatalities. In 2021, 33% of all fatalities and 31.2% of 
all serious injuries resulted from distracted driving crashes. Distracted driving citations issued 
have risen each year since 2020. 
 
Farm Equipment 
 
Farming is an important part of Caroline County’s economy and rural heritage. The safe and 
efficient movement of farm equipment along County highways and roads is critical for the farm- 
based economy. The development of transportation-related plans, policies, and regulations 
should include Farm Bureau representatives and remain cognizant of the needs of the farming 
industry as a whole in relation to the planning for transportation routes. This includes appropriate 
rights-of-way for farm equipment as well as signage that vehicular traffic is entering an agricultural 
area. 
 
Public Transportation 
 
Delmarva Community Transit (DCT), the current transportation provider for Caroline County, 
provides limited public transit in for Kent, Caroline, Talbot and Dorchester Counties. Queen 
Anne’s County Ride provides limited transit in Queen Anne’s and Talbot Counties. DCT 
participates in Maryland Upper Shore Transit (MUST), which is a collaborative effort between 
Upper Shore Transportation providers to operate regional fixed routes. DCT as a part of Delmarva 
Community Services, Inc., also provides transportation to the elderly and disabled to help them 
live a more independent lifestyle, through American Disabilities Act (ADA) and Medical Assistance 
(MA) programs. Additionally, they provide demand-response coverage to areas where there are 
no fixed routes. 
 
DCT developed a Transit Development Plan for Caroline, Kent and Talbot Counties in April of 
2022. This report presents the Transit Development Plan (TDP) to provide guidance for the 
provision of public transit services over the five-year period from fiscal years 2022/2023 to 2027. 
The Plan provides a roadmap for the three counties and their transit provider – Delmarva 
Community Transit (DCT) – for implementing service changes and improvements while also 
meeting the requirements established by Maryland Transit Authority (MTA), a division of the 
Maryland Department of Transportation (MDOT). DCT provides fixed routes and specialized 
transportation in the three counties. The agency also coordinates with County Ride, the local 
transit service in Queen Anne’s County, operating transportation services in Maryland’s Upper 
and Mid Shore regions are branded as Maryland Upper Shore Transit (MUST). The two 
transportation providers operate flex routes with service in their home counties, as well as Talbot, 
Caroline, and Kent counties and with a route to Salisbury in Wicomico County and a route to 
Annapolis in Anne Arundel County. 
 
The TDP identified transit issues and unmet needs provided below: 
 
• 
Real-time transit information and participation in Google Transit which is a feature within 
Google Maps that allows users to view public transportation options. DCS is in the process 
of implementing this improvement.

<!-- page 134 -->
121 
 
• 
A systemwide map is needed, as well as additional marketing. 
• 
Improved bus stop amenities. 
• 
Expanded hours for satellite offices. 
• 
The development of employer-based programs should be explored. 
• 
The system may need to plan for additional transportation services to address mental 
health issues that have grown during the pandemic. 
• 
The population in the region is aging with a projection of 27% of the population in Caroline 
County being 60+ years old by 2030 – this will affect future transit services. 
• 
It is important to ensure that areas with above average percentage of racial and/or ethnic 
minorities are not disproportionately impacted by any proposed alterations to existing 
public transportation services. Caroline County has the highest concentration of minority 
population with the highest in Denton and Federalsburg. 
• 
Individuals who earn less than the federal poverty level face financial hardships and may 
be more likely to depend on public transportation. The highest-density area with the 
greatest concentration of low-income populations is found in Greensboro. 
• 
Limited English Proficiency (LEP) population is a count of people who do not speak 
English as their primary language and their ability to speak English is less than “very well”. 
• 
Spanish is the most prevalent language that non-English speaking area residents speak 
with Caroline County having the most Spanish speaking residents. 
• 
Identify the locations of popular destinations including educational facilities, human 
service agencies, major employers, medical facilities, and shopping centers. 
 
The Economic Development Director of Caroline County indicated that a lack of transportation for 
employees is an important issue for Caroline County employers as well as the significant 
challenges to the region, most notably associated with grouping trips in such a rural area. 
 
The outreach to the public, riders, and public transportation stakeholders reiterated many of the 
challenges and unmet transportation needs discussed by TDP committee members at the 
beginning of the TDP process. Common themes articulated by these stakeholder groups are 
summarized below. 
 
• 
More community awareness of DCT is needed, as well improved access to information. 
The public survey revealed that 46% of the respondents were not familiar with DCT. In 
addition, a comment on the public survey requested that information be more widely 
distributed in paper format throughout the community for people who may not be able to 
access the information electronically.       
• 
DCT services are vital to the people who use them, with almost 35% indicating that they 
would not be able to travel to their needed destinations without DCT. 
• 
Additional capacity is needed, as well as additional service on Saturdays and later hours 
of service on weekdays. Sunday service is also needed. 
• 
Innovative service delivery methods should be explored, including the use of microtransit 
and the implementation of shared scheduling (with Shore Regional Health) to streamline 
trips for medical appointments. 
• 
Improved coordination among the agencies that serve the disadvantaged population in

<!-- page 135 -->
122 
 
the region is needed. 
 
One potential project for DCT is exploring rural microtransit services. While not ideal for very 
remote areas, microtransit could be effective in towns. These services use smaller vehicles and 
mobile apps for on-demand, dynamically routed rides within a specific zone, offering more 
flexibility and efficiency than traditional fixed-route or demand-response systems. 
 
Ride-share programs should be encouraged in areas with limited access to public transportation. 
Local communities should be encouraged and supported in developing plans to implement and 
promote these initiatives. 
 
Caroline County will continue collaborating with public transportation providers to identify service 
gaps, unmet needs, and support the development of effective, accessible solutions. Expanding 
public transportation options not only benefits residents but also contributes to the overall well- 
being of the County. Reducing vehicle miles traveled – through ridesharing, public transit, or 
consolidated trips – helps decrease road wear and tear, lowers transportation-related emissions, 
and minimizes impacts to the environment. 
 
Alternative Modes of Transportation 
 
According to the County’s Land Preservation, Parks and Recreation Plan (LPPRP), there are 
miles of biking, walking and riding trails throughout the County. Trails in Tuckahoe State Park link 
with trails in the Adkins Arboretum allowing greater access to resources by alternative modes of 
transportation. Martinak State Park and the Idylwild Wildlife Management Area also provide 
extensive trail networks. Additionally, there are Maryland State Highway Signed Bicycle Routes 
that span the County to allow for alternative modes of transportation (see map 6-1 showing the 
Maryland Department of Transportation’s Bicycle Route Map for Caroline County). State-owned 
Park land consists of 1,511 acres; County parks provide an additional 445 acres for walking, 
biking and other park-related activities. 
 
Public access to rivers and lakes for boating and fishing is an important component of the County’s 
recreation facilities and tourism. The network of public water access sites includes boat landings 
and waterfront parks owned by the State, County and municipal governments. 
 
 
Transportation Implementation 
 
• 
County departments will provide input as needed to the Department of Public Works to identify 
and prioritize the maintenance and improvement needs of the County roads and bridges and 
their stormwater and flood management systems for future construction, upgrades, and/or 
improvements and track progress of ongoing projects. 
 
• 
Continued efforts focused on supporting providers and improving public access to 
transportation options throughout the County.

<!-- page 136 -->
123 
 
• 
Support tourism transportation initiatives that will be beneficial to the County. 
 
• 
Support completion of the dualization of Maryland Route 404 and all priority State Road 
improvement projects as listed by the County, MDOT and SHA. 
 
• 
With the update to the Zoning Code, review and consider implementing additional regulations 
and mitigation requirements for high impact road use projects (commercial, industrial, 
institutional and high-density residential) to address during the development review process.

<!-- page 137 -->
124 
 
 
For the purposes of this Comprehensive Plan, community facilities in Caroline County are defined 
to include recreation and parks facilities; educational facilities, libraries, museums and cultural 
resources; emergency services and public health facilities; law enforcement and court facilities; 
and public works facilities. 
 
A primary goal of the Caroline County Comprehensive Plan is to provide and maintain a system 
of community facilities that effectively meets the changing needs of County residents. This system 
should be planned and managed in a manner that supports and aligns with the County’s land use 
and growth management goals and objectives.  
 
Objectives for community facilities include: 
 
• 
To promote effective interagency coordination with the appropriate Municipal, County, and 
State agencies and entities in the planning, development, and programming of community 
facilities that align with the guiding principles, goals, and long-term vision outlined in the 
Comprehensive Plan; 
• 
To assess and provide sufficient parklands, open space lands and recreation facilities that are 
available to meet both current community needs and projected population growth; 
• 
To ensure the provision of a safe, reliable supply of potable water and adequate wastewater 
treatment for all County residents; 
• 
To encourage municipalities to annex adjacent areas that lack adequate public water and 
sewer services to promote efficient infrastructure expansion; 
• 
To concentrate growth within Priority Funding Areas (PFAs) to maximize the use of existing 
or planned public infrastructure, reduce sprawl, and promote fiscally and environmentally 
sustainable development;  
• 
To ensure that all proposed development is contingent upon the availability and capacity of essential 
public facilities and services, including but not limited to roads, water supply, wastewater treatment 
and schools; 
• 
To coordinate with public and private entities to site and design public facilities in a manner 
that supports the County’s and municipalities’ growth management objectives and; 
• 
To guide the development of communications infrastructure in a way that strengthens 
emergency capabilities and enhances broadband access to support business growth and 
community connectivity. 
 
Maryland Department of Planning (MDP) projects a total of 38,720 residents by 2050 for Caroline 
County which represents an average annual growth rate of 1.2%. Population growth and 
demographic shifts in the County will influence the nature, location, and scale of community facilities 
and services required to meet future needs.   
 
To support a growing population, County and Municipal governments must engage in the planned 
and coordinated assessment, development, and expansion of adequate community facilities and

services. Key areas include public water and sewer, parks and recreation facilities and services, 
public education facilities, and public safety services such as emergency management, fire, 
medical, police, and disaster response. See Table 7-1 for an inventory of existing community 
facilities and Map 7-1 for their locations.

Recreation and Parks Facilities 
 
In 2022, the Caroline County Land Preservation, Parks & Recreation Plan (LPPRP) was updated. 
Updates to the LPPRP are required under State law every six years. 
The Caroline County LPPRP is developed to assist the State’s evaluation of each County’s land 
preservation and recreation program to ensure adequate public investment. Most importantly, the 
LPPRP qualifies local governments for Maryland Program Open Space (POS) funding/grants. 
The LPPRP serves as a guide for park acquisition, land preservation and conservation for the 
County and its municipalities. The LPPRP provides detailed strategies for recreation, land 
preservation, and natural resource conservation that extend beyond the scope of the County’s 
Comprehensive Plan. 
 
The LPPRP aligns with and supports the preservation and conservation goals outlined in the 
Comprehensive Plan. The “Mission Statement” of the LPPRP is to “improve and enrich citizen 
well-being and community life through the provision of affordable, high quality activities and parks 
and facilities for Caroline County families and individuals.”   
 
The LPPRP outlines significant strategies for advancing recreation and parks, preserving 
agricultural land, and conserving natural resources within the County. One key strategy is the

<!-- page 142 -->
129 
 
 
strengthening of mandatory open space dedication requirements within the County subdivision 
regulations. Effective May 1, 2007, the County began requiring all major subdivisions include 
accessible recreational or open space equal to at least five percent of the subdivisions gross area, 
with a minimum area and parcel size of not less than three- quarters of an acre.  
 
Additionally: 
 
• 
At least seventy-five percent of the required or open space must be suitable for active 
recreation.  
• 
All required recreation and open space must be offered for public dedication.  
• 
A major update to the open space regulations was the exclusion of environmentally 
constrained areas in required open space calculations, ensuring that the designated land 
is suitable for recreational activity.  
 
As shown in Table 7-2, the County currently owns: 
 
• 
158 acres of recreation land, and 
• 
324 acres of resource land. 
 
Recreation land is developed for primary use by the public; resource lands are lands whose 
primary objective is natural resource protection. The majority of the public recreation and resource 
land is State-owned, comprising 7,272 acres.  An additional 1,011 acres are owned by the 
County, towns, and Board of Education (BOE). As of 2024, including public and private/quasi-
public lands, the County has a total of 10,512 acres of recreation and resource land. 
 
Caroline County’s municipalities play a vital role in supporting recreation and parks throughout 
the County. Approximately 44% of the County’s recreation land is located within the incorporated 
towns, highlighting the significant contribution of municipal areas to the overall recreational 
network. Municipalities often serve as primary points of contact for the public, offering accessible 
recreational facilities and programming. Many of the recommended future land acquisitions and 
recreation projects identified in County planning efforts are located in or near municipalities.

LPPRP Recreational Attributes 
 
Caroline County categorizes its recreation and resource lands into a diverse range of park types 
and facilities to serve varying community needs.  These include: 
 
• 
Regional parks  
• 
Community parks 
• 
Neighborhood parks 
• 
School recreation parks and fitness facilities 
• 
Mini parks 
• 
Special use areas 
• 
Water access sites  
• 
Undeveloped parks  
 
State-owned recreation and resource lands are located throughout the County: 
 
• 
Tuckahoe State Park (primarily undeveloped and the largest State recreation holding) 
• 
Chesapeake Forest/State Forest Area 
• 
Martinak State Park 
• 
Idyllwild Natural Resource Area 
• 
Denton Armory

<!-- page 144 -->
131 
 
There are no Federally owned recreation lands located within Caroline County, however the 
Harriet Tubman Underground Railroad (UGRR) Byway, a driving tour developed by the National 
Park Service in conjunction with the Harriet Tubman Underground Railroad National Historical 
Park in Dorchester County, includes several locations throughout Caroline County  
 
The Town of Denton is the first officially recognized “Trail Town” along the American Discovery 
Trail. This national trail, created in 1990 and managed by the non-profit American Discovery Trail 
Society, spans approximately 6,800 miles across 15 states from coast to coast and showcases 
the diverse landscapes and cultural heritage of the United States.  It is the only coast-to-coast, 
non-motorized trail in the United States. Denton’s designation as a Trail Town will provide an 
economic boost in tourism with hikers, bikers, and adventure seekers bringing new business to 
local shops, dining and lodging.  This is also an opportunity to promote local history and culture 
to a national audience. 
 
Water Access 
 
Caroline County provides public water access at four major landing sites: 
 
• 
Hillsboro Boat Ramp (Tuckahoe River) – County owned 
• 
Choptank Marina (Choptank River) – County owned 
• 
Ganey’s Wharf (Choptank River) – County owned 
• 
 Greensboro Boat Ramp (Choptank River) – Leased and operated by the County, owned 
by the Greensboro Fire Department 
 
Additional public landings and/or marinas are available in:  
• 
Denton 
• 
Federalsburg 
• 
Martinak and Tuckahoe State parks 
• 
Smithville Community Lake 
 
These water access points contribute significantly to recreational opportunities such as boating, 
fishing, and passive recreation (hiking, birdwatching, etc.) activities across the County. 
 
LPPRP Capital Improvement Program 
 
The 2022 LPPRP includes a Long Range Capital Improvement Program with a nine-year 
timeframe. Most of the County’s priorities within that period are enhancement projects at existing 
facilities, including the North and South County regional parks, community parks within 
municipalities and rural villages, and waterfront recreational facilities such as Choptank Marina 
and Ganey’s Wharf.

Educational Facilities 
 
As stated in the 2024 Educational Facilities Master Plan for Caroline County Public Schools, 
enrollment in Caroline County public schools is projected to increase significantly in the next 10 
to 15 years, with 4 schools slated to be near or over State-rated capacity limits by 2030. The 
County and the Board of Education will continue to work together to ensure that the capacity for 
educational facilities is considered during the development approval process.  
 
Most of Caroline County’s public schools received facility upgrades and renovations between 
2010 and 2025. Projects included roof replacements, security and safety upgrades, infrastructure 
improvements and internal building alterations. Greensboro Elementary School was entirely 
replaced with a new building in 2021.

The educational facilities in Caroline County are well-maintained and offer a clean and safe 
environment for learning.  However, several aging school buildings require modernization to 
support the continued delivery of high-quality programs. Future modernization projects will be 
necessary at several of the County’s older school facilities, including Denton Elementary, Ridgely 
Elementary, Federalsburg Elementary, Lockerman Middle School, and the Caroline Career and 
Technology Center.   The timing and implementation of these projects must be balanced with 
available local funding and the potential need for additional student capacity.  
 
Current capacity issues are most acute at Greensboro Elementary, which was built in 2021 and 
in 2025 is already at 100% capacity, and Ridgley Elementary, which is currently at 95% capacity. 
Future enrollment projections indicate Denton Elementary will be near 95% capacity within the 
next 10 years.  Lockerman Middle School and North Caroline High School also have capacity 
pressures that will increase within the next 10 years. 
 
The Superintendent and Board of Education have recognized the importance of preparing a 
proactive long-range plan to address both school modernization and capacity needs.  This 
planning effort has included policy updates, such as the adoption of the Long Range Facility 
Planning and Student School Assignment policies.  Additionally, the school system has developed 
growth scenarios and adjusted its Capital Improvement Program (CIP) to cover a 10-year 
planning horizon, rather than the traditionally required 5 years.  This extended timeline provides 
a clearer understanding of the total fiscal impact associated with both ongoing modernization 
efforts and the need for additional capacity.
