Economic Development Initiatives 
 
Major economic development initiatives in Caroline County focus on enhancing regional 
infrastructure and services, achieving economies of scale, expanding tourism opportunities and 
fostering the growth of new industry opportunities. These strategic and coordinated efforts are 
expected to significantly improve the economic prospects and long-term competitiveness for 
Caroline County. 
 
Infrastructure includes roads, bridges, water and sewer systems, as well as technological 
infrastructure such as fiber optics and broadband. Adequate and modern infrastructure is 
essential to attract new businesses and support economic growth.  In addition to physical assets, 
access to reliable public and private services – such as healthcare, education, and workforce 
development – is also critical to make the County an attractive location for business investment.  
 
Regional “economies of scale” strengthen interjurisdictional connections across counties, 
municipalities, and state and federal agencies by maximizing shared investments and reducing 
long-term costs. New economic development councils assist in facilitating regionalism. In 
Caroline, Dorchester, and Talbot Counties the regional economic development entity is the Mid-
Shore Regional Council. The County helped the Council develop the Midshore Comprehensive 
Economic Development Strategy (CEDS). Among the projects in the action plan were the North 
County Wastewater Treatment Plant and Sewer Collection System, North County Enterprise Zone 
in 2018 consisting of 710 acres covering Ridgley, Greensboro, Goldsboro, and the Mid- Shore 
Regional Technology and Business Park.  According to data provided by Maryland Office of 
Statewide Broadband less than 2% of Caroline County broadband serviceable areas are 
considered unserved or underserved.  Other broadband providers have expanded in the County 
and provide services to customers located in municipalities.  Caroline County Commissioners 
Office partners with Maryland Department of Information Technology, Maryland Broadband 
Cooperative and Choptank Fiber to share fiber-optic resources.   
 
A variety of incentives are available to attract new businesses, motivate existing businesses to 
relocate and support the growth and retention of existing ones.  These incentives should be 
implemented and utilized to maximize the opportunities for business growth and retention.  
 
Non-Financial Incentives: 
• 
Streamlined permitting and licensing – Simplify applications and approval processes to 
reduce costs and delays for businesses. 
• 
Technical assistance and training – Facilitate workshops, mentoring, and business 
advisory services by partnering with established companies to provide expertise and 
networking opportunities.  These initiatives would promote workforce development that 
can support the hiring needs of existing companies along with helping entrepreneurs to 
improve skills, learn business operations, and provide business relationships. 
• 
Educational training – Offer training sessions focused on navigating funding available, to 
identify and apply for state and federal grants, as well as available tax credits for

<!-- page 188 -->
175 
 
businesses.  Provide guidance on financial assistance programs and understanding 
eligibility requirements.  Additionally, conduct sessions that help entrepreneurs and small 
business owners understand County regulations, zoning requirements, and permitting 
processes – for traditional business locations and in-home occupations. 
• 
Networking opportunities – Facilitate connections through business meetups, local 
Chamber of Commerce, or other business groups. 
• 
Marketing support – Promote local businesses throughout community websites, social 
media, or local events. 
• 
Access to Public Spaces – Provide free or low-cost access to public venues for pop-up 
shops, markets, or community events. 
• 
Recognition Programs – Create awards or certification programs to highlight and promote 
local businesses. 
• 
Zoning Code Update – Review the permitted uses and associated conditions for 
commercial businesses and consider incorporating additional predefined conditions for 
certain businesses that currently require review and approval by the Board of Zoning 
Appeals that could be approved administratively, provided they meet the established 
conditions. 
 
Low-Cost Financial Incentives: 
• 
Fee waivers or reductions – Reduce or waive local fees for licenses, permits or other 
required items for new or expanding businesses. 
• 
Tax deferrals – Allow businesses to defer certain local taxes temporarily to assist start up 
and ease cash flow. 
• 
Facilitate access to external funding – Help businesses with state or federal grant 
programs, nonprofit loans, or crowdfunding platforms. 
 
Community-based initiatives: 
• 
Business incubation support – Partner with nonprofits, universities, or regional 
organizations to provide shared workspace and resources at a low cost or at no cost. 
• 
Volunteer support – Engage local professionals and retirees to volunteer time as mentors 
or advisors. 
• 
Collaborative purchasing – Help small businesses band together to purchase supplies or 
services in bulk at discounted rates. 
 
Local workforce initiatives: 
• 
Educational opportunities and programs – Currently available through the public schools 
and the local college, these should be regularly reviewed and expanded to support a wider 
range of industries and careers.  It’s essential to ensure not just availability but accessibility 
so that all have the opportunity to participate and benefit.  
• 
Apprenticeship and internship programs - Expanding existing opportunities and creating 
new ones can strengthen workforce readiness by connecting students and job seekers 
with hands-on experience, career training, and direct connections to local businesses and

<!-- page 189 -->
176 
 
industries. These efforts also help retain young talent after graduation and support the 
staffing needs of local businesses. 
 
Tourism is a vital industry that supports small local businesses in Caroline County.  Recent efforts 
to improve tourism in Caroline County have focused on promoting the County’s rich history – most 
notably through the reopening of the Linchester Mill as a historical destination.  A nature trail has 
been developed behind the mill providing nature pathways, linking visitors to historic Underground 
Railroad crossing routes and is the first Braille signed trail.  Picnic tables have been added, and 
special events are hosted throughout the year. Additionally, the development of scenic byways 
aims to attract visitors by highlighting the natural beauty and cultural heritage of the region. 
 
The Chesapeake Culinary Center - also known as Caroline Culinary Arts – was initiated by the 
Friends of the Grape, Inc., partnered with the Denton Development Corporation, Town of Denton, 
Caroline County, and Caroline County Board of Education to provide training opportunities for 
high school students and adults in the food service/tourism industry.  It is a vibrant nonprofit 
culinary hub providing culinary education, community classes, a community kitchen for local 
entrepreneurs and farmers, along with a catering service.  It is housed in the beautifully restored, 
historic Caroline County High School building that was originally built around 1901.  In 2016, the 
Maryland Historical Trust awarded it the Project Excellence: Partnerships award for outstanding 
historic preservation efforts.  
Denton was officially designated as the first American Discovery Trail “Trail Town” with a formal 
recognition by the American Discovery Trail Society for its unique relationship with the coast-to-
coast Great American Discovery Trail. This emphasizes its role in supporting hikers, cyclists and 
equestrians along the roughly 6,800 mile national trail spanning 15 states from Delaware to 
California and will support increased tourism and trail-based recreation. 
 
There should be a continued and expanded investment in arts and cultural programs that 
showcase local and regional artists – including live music performances – along with more annual 
events, festivals, and holiday-themed celebrations, all which will engage locals and attract new 
visitors. 
 
The County should plan unincorporated growth near municipalities and invest in infrastructure 
and public services to support that growth. Growth within and around municipalities can attract 
new employers, stimulate the development of local businesses – particularly in downtown areas 
– and increase demand for service-sector jobs.  Additionally, a growing population may boost the 
agricultural economy through an increased demand in locally grown produce and locally made 
goods. Strengthening the local economy may also attract or retain a younger population that will 
broaden the tax base needed to support essential public services, particularly those required by 
a growing elderly population. 
 
Economic Development Implementation 
 
• 
To support and strengthen both existing and new businesses, the County should conduct

<!-- page 190 -->
177 
 
regular business needs assessments through surveys and focus groups to better understand 
local challenges and opportunities.  Provide financial incentives such as grants, low-interest 
loans, and tax breaks to help businesses grow and remain viable.  Streamline permitting and 
licensing processes to reduce administrative and time burdens making it easier for businesses 
to open, operate, and expand.  Additionally, the development of business incubators, shared 
workspaces and “pop-up” opportunities will foster entrepreneurship and provide critical 
support for startups and small-scale enterprises. 
 
• 
Partner with economic development organizations to attract businesses in high-wage sectors. 
Evaluate the full range of available incentive options and implement all that will best support 
business growth and community development. Promote infrastructure investments – such as 
broadband expansion, utility upgrades, and transportation improvements – to ensure the 
County is well equipped to support new business development and long-term economic 
growth. 
 
• 
To strengthen the local workface and meet industry needs, the County will partner with 
Caroline County Public Schools, community colleges, the municipalities and regional 
employers to deliver targeted training programs in high-demand fields such as healthcare, 
manufacturing, trades, and technology.  The County should target funding for apprenticeships, 
internships, and on-the-job training programs through formal partnerships with local 
businesses, while investing in modern workforce training facilities.  Hosting regular employer 
roundtables to identify current and future workforce skill gaps to ensure training programs 
align with industry needs.  Develop and maintain a centralized job and training resource portal 
for residents and businesses. 
 
• 
To attract visitors who contribute to the local economy while minimizing long-term impacts on 
the community, the County should develop and market unique local events, festivals, and 
cultural experiences throughout the year to attract both residents and visitors in every season. 
The County should coordinate with the municipalities to align efforts in planning, promoting   
Expand and enhance existing events and festivals with additional activities, diverse vendors 
and interactive experiences to increase community participation, support local businesses and 
attract both local residents and out-of-town visitors. Invest in tourism infrastructure (signage, 
parking) that supports short-term visits.  Encourage development of lodging, dining, and retail 
businesses that serve tourists.  Promote agritourism, history and eco-tourism experiences that 
align with rural and natural settings. Collaborate with regional tourism boards to amplify 
marketing efforts, broaden outreach, and attract a wider range of visitors. 
 
• 
Protect prime farmland through conservation easements and zoning policies that preserve 
land for long-term agricultural use.  Encourage agricultural related business ventures like 
processing, packaging and farm-to-table enterprises. Facilitate access to markets through 
regional food hubs and cooperative networks. Promote water and soil conservation practices 
that ensure long-term land productivity for future generations.

<!-- page 191 -->
178 
 
• 
Create incentives for businesses that source raw materials or ingredients locally through tax 
deductions or credits.  Support branding and certification programs and create a recognizable 
badge or certification for businesses that meet a local sourcing threshold.  This helps increase 
visibility and consumer trust in local products. Assist in connecting producers with retailers, 
restaurants, and institutions along with promoting local goods at farmers markets, craft fairs, 
and online marketplaces. 
 
• 
With the review of the Zoning Code, ensure that zoning designations and requirements align 
with current and future land use goals and establish clear zoning regulations that permit 
mixed-use development where appropriate. Engage stakeholders in land use planning to 
balance economic goals with community character. Maintain buffer zones and allow flexibility 
to increase buffer areas and dimensions as needed to reduce conflicts between industrial, 
commercial and residential uses. 
 
• 
As part of the review of the Zoning Code and the Zoning Maps, evaluate if there is sufficient 
industrial and commercial zoned land – including areas near railroad corridors – to attract 
businesses. Align infrastructure development – such as roads, public water and sewer, 
telecommunications, and other utilities – with long-term planning.  
 
• 
Increase the County’s economic development staff and budget to lead and support 
collaboration with the towns and municipalities.  A formal partnership framework should be 
established to coordinate water and sewer expansion projects and align infrastructure with 
business and tourism development.  Tourism efforts should focus on attracting visitors who 
contribute to the local economy short-term stays and day trips, minimizing long-term impacts 
on infrastructure, schools, and public services.  Joint marketing efforts, shared incentives, and 
coordinated outreach will help attract new businesses, and promote the region.  Progress will 
be tracked through measurable outcomes and reported annually to all partners.

<!-- page 192 -->
179 
 
 
This implementation framework establishes strategies and performance measures that will guide the execution, monitoring, and 
evaluation of the Comprehensive Plan over time.  It provides the structure for translating the Plan’s visions, goals, policies, and actions 
into measurable outcomes by identifying priorities, assigning responsibilities, tracking progress, and promoting accountability. 
 
Successful implementation is essential to achieving the County’s long-term objectives.  The strategies and performance measures 
provide a structured approach for assessing progress, identifying challenges, and ensuring that public investments, regulations, 
programs, and initiatives remain aligned with the Plan’s goals. Some of the implementation actions provided below are summarized.  
Refer to each chapter for the complete goals, objectives, and specific implementation language. 
 
Recognizing that conditions and priorities will evolve, this framework is designed to be flexible and adaptive.  Regular monitoring and 
evaluation will allow the County to adjust the priorities, timelines, and implementation strategies in response to changing economic 
conditions, demographic and workforce trends, infrastructure needs, funding opportunities, technological advancements, and evolving 
community priorities.  Through this ongoing process, the County can ensure that the Comprehensive Plan remains a relevant and 
effective guide for future decision-making. 
 
 
CHAPTER 1: LAND USE AND DEVELOPMENT 
The goals and objectives of the Land Use Chapter establish the guidance for future growth, development, preservation, and conservation 
throughout Caroline County.  Implementation of these goals and objectives will occur primarily through a comprehensive update of the 
Caroline County Zoning Code and the Zoning Maps following adoption of the Comprehensive Plan.  Key implementation efforts will focus 
on protecting agricultural land and rural character, supporting orderly and sustainable growth patterns, preserving residential and village 
areas for appropriate development, conserving valuable natural and historic resources, and strengthening coordination between the 
County and municipalities on land use, infrastructure, and growth management initiatives.

CHAPTER 2: RESOURCE CONSERVATION 
The goals and objectives of the Resource Conservation Chapter focus on protecting, managing, and conserving Caroline County’s natural 
and environmental resources.  Priorities include limiting development in sensitive areas, preserving habitats for rare and endangered 
species, conserving forest and woodland resources, and protecting valuable agricultural soils.

CHAPTER 3: WATER RESOURCES 
The goals and objectives of the Water Resources chapter focus on protecting and maintaining safe and adequate drinking water supplies, 
improving water quality and conserving the County’s waterways and aquatic habitats.  Implementation efforts will emphasize protecting 
water resources from incompatible land uses and pollution, supporting watershed restoration initiatives, managing stormwater runoff 
through green infrastructure practices, and utilizing the County’s Zoning Code, Comprehensive Water and Sewer Plan, and watershed 
planning efforts to guide long-term water resource protection.

CHAPTER 4: MINERAL RESOURCES 
The goals and objectives of the Mineral Resources Chapter focus on managing and conserving Caroline County’s mineral resources 
while protecting surrounding land uses, infrastructure, agricultural operations, and environmental resources from the impacts of mining 
activities.  Implementation efforts emphasize responsible extraction practices, protection of residential and agricultural areas, 
preservation of scenic and rural character, maintenance of critical drainage systems, and coordination with the Maryland Department of 
the Environment to ensure appropriate reclamation, bonding and mitigation measure are in place.

CHAPTER 5: PRIORITY PRESERVATION 
The goals and objectives for the Priority Preservation Area are to protect farmland from non-agricultural development.  Key strategies 
include securing additional funding for agricultural preservation easements, advocating for policies that minimize impacts from State 
mandates that encourage and require the conversion of farmland to non-agriculture development and uses, updating County regulations 
to support farm-related economic opportunities, prioritizing the protection of prime farmland, improving coordination among governmental 
and private partners, and continuing support for land preservation and conservation programs.

CHAPTER 6: TRANSPORTATION 
The goals and objectives of the Transportation element are to ensure the safe, efficient, and coordinated movement of people and goods 
throughout the County.  Key objectives include maintaining and improving road, bridge, stormwater, and flood management infrastructure; 
requiring transportation improvements associated with major developments projects; coordinating local transportation planning with 
County, State, and Federal agencies; supporting expanded public transportation options; and promoting pedestrian and bicycle facilities 
within Priority Funding Areas and designated growth areas.

CHAPTER 7: COMMUNITY FACILITIES 
The goals and objectives of the Community Facilities chapter focus on providing and maintaining public facilities and services that meet 
the needs of current and future residents while supporting the County’s growth management goals. The plan emphasizes coordination 
among municipal, County, State, and private entities in the planning and development of community facilities. It seeks to ensure adequate 
parks, recreation areas, open space, water supply, and wastewater treatment services, while directing growth to Priority Funding Areas 
where infrastructure can be used most efficiently.  The element also promotes development only where essential public facilities and 
services are available and supports the expansion of communications infrastructure to improve emergency response capabilities, 
broadband access, and economic opportunities.

CHAPTER 8: HOUSING 
The goals and objectives of the Housing chapter focus on ensuring that Caroline County provides a sufficient supply of safe, sanitary, 
affordable, and diverse housing opportunities for current and future residents.  It addresses the challenges of rising housing costs, 
regulatory requirements, infrastructure limitations, and changing demographic needs while promoting sustainable residential growth.  The 
objectives are to direct housing development to appropriate growth areas, expand housing choices, preserve and improve existing housing 
stock, streamline development and permitting processes, address infrastructure constraints, protect residential communities, strengthen 
public-private partnerships, and regularly monitor housing needs and trends.  Collectively, these strategies are intended to increase 
housing availability and affordability, support economic growth, and maintain the quality of life and rural character of Caroline County’s 
communities.

CHAPTER 9: ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT 
The goals and objectives of the Economic Development chapter are to foster a strong, diverse, and sustainable economy that improves 
residents’ quality of life while preserving Caroline County’s rural and agricultural character.  The chapter emphasizes supporting the 
growth and success of existing and new businesses, attracting higher-wage employment opportunities, and strengthening workforce 
development through partnerships with schools, employers, and regional institutions.  It promotes strategic economic growth within 
designated development areas, coordinated infrastructure investments, and adequate commercial and industrial land to support 
business expansion.  The chapter also highlights the importance of agriculture as a key economic driver, encourages industries that 
utilize locally produced goods, and supports tourism initiatives that leverage the County’s natural, cultural, and historical resources.  
Through collaboration among the County, municipalities, educational institutions, and the private sector, the chapter provides a 
framework for building a resilient economy that supports long-term prosperity for residents, businesses, and the agricultural community.

195 
 
 
 
To maintain accountability and support informed decision-making, the County departments, municipalities, groups and other partners 
shall provide information for inclusion in an Annual Comprehensive Plan Implementation Report to be presented to the County 
Commissioners.  The report shall summarize completed actions, evaluate progress toward achieving the Plan’s goals and objectives, 
identify challenges and resource needs, and recommend future priorities and timelines.  The report shall be presented in advance of the 
County’s annual budget and Capital Improvement Program review process to ensure that funding decisions reflect the priorities and 
implementation needs identified in the Comprehensive Plan.    
 
The first Annual Comprehensive Plan Implementation Report shall be completed by December 2028 and presented to the County 
Commissioners no later than February 2029. Subsequent report shall be completed and presented annually thereafter.
